A strange five vertex model and multispecies ASEP on a ring
Atsuo Kuniba, Masato Okado, Travis Scrimshaw

Abstract
. We revisit the problem of constructing the stationary states of the multispecies asymmetric simple exclusion process on a one-dimensional periodic lattice. Central to our approach is a quantum oscillator weighted five vertex model which features a strange weight conservation distinct from the conventional one. Our results clarify the interrelations among several known results and refine their derivations. For instance, the stationary probability derived from the multiline queue construction by Martin (2020) and Corteel–Mandelshtam–Williams (2022) is identified with the partition function of a three-dimensional system. The matrix product operators by Prolhac–Evans–Mallick (2009) acquire a natural diagrammatic interpretation as corner transfer matrices (CTM). The origin of their recursive tensor structure, as questioned by Aggarwal–Nicoletti–Petrov (2023), is revealed through the CTM diagrams. Finally, the derivation of the Zamolodchikov–Faddeev algebra by Cantini–de Gier–Wheeler (2015) is made intrinsic by elucidating its precise connection to a solution to the Yang–Baxter equation originating from quantum group representations.



1.
Introduction

The asymmetric simple exclusion process (ASEP)
[
MGP68
,
Spi70
]
is a fundamental model of non-equilibrium stochastic dynamics with many applications in physics, biology, probability theory, and other scientific fields.
In recent years, it has been extensively studied, particularly in one dimension, leading to a variety of generalizations and a wealth of results that intersect with statistical mechanics, algebraic combinatorics, special functions, integrable systems,
representation theory, etc.
See for example
[
AKSS09
,
ANP23
,
BW22
,
CdGW15
,
CMW22
,
CRV14
,
Mar20
,
PEM09
]
and the references therein.

In this paper, we consider the standard continuous time
n
-species ASEP on a periodic lattice of length
L
.
Each local state is selected from
\{0,1,\ldots,n\}
, where
1,\ldots,n
represent the presence of one of the
n
species of particles, and
0
corresponds to an empty site.
The model includes a parameter
t
that determines the asymmetry of the nearest-neighbor hopping rates.
The first significant problem is to construct a stationary state, which is unique within each sector specified by the particle content.
The problem is trivial for
n=1
, where all the possible states are equally probable.

The multispecies case
n\geq 2
is non-trivial and has been solved in two intriguing ways: combinatorially and algebraically.
The combinatorial approach is known
as the multiline queue (MLQ) construction
[
CMW22
,
Mar20
]
,
while the algebraic method is based on the
Zamolodchikov–Faddeev (ZF) algebra
[
CdGW15
,
PEM09
]
.
The latter directly leads to the matrix product formula for the (unnormalized)
stationary probability of the configuration
(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{L})\in\{0,\ldots,n\}^{L}
:

\mathbb{P}(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{L})=\tr(X_{\sigma_{1}}\cdots X_{\sigma_{L}}).

Here
X_{0},\ldots,X_{n}
are operators acting on some auxiliary space over which the trace is taken.
It is well known that the above formula is valid if there are (not necessarily unique)
spectral parameter dependent versions

X_{0}(z),\ldots,X_{n}(z)
satisfying the ZF algebra whose structure function
is a stochastic
R
matrix related to the Markov matrix of the
n
-ASEP.

Central in our approach is a certain five vertex model
on the two-dimensional square lattice whose “Boltzmann weights”
take values in a
t
-deformed quantum oscillator algebra acting on its bosonic Fock space.
We call it the
t
-oscillator weighted five vertex model. See (
54
).
A curious feature is that it does
not
satisfy the usual weight conservation
or the so-called “ice condition” as in the six vertex model
[
Bax89
]
, nor does it satisfy the Yang–Baxter equation.
However, the model can also be interpreted as a three-dimensional system, where the Fock space is attached to the edges in the third direction.

The strange five vertex model plays a pivotal role, refining many known results
[
CdGW15
,
CMW22
,
Mar20
,
PEM09
]
and synthesizing their techniques together.
Despite not satisfying the Yang–Baxter equation, it still presents many beneficial aspects:

•

The operators
X_{0}(z),\ldots,X_{n}(z)
are formulated as
corner transfer matrices
(CTM) à la Baxter (cf.
[
Bax89
, Chap.13]
).

•

These CTM diagrams immediately lead to the recursion relation of these operators with respect to
n
.

•

Stationary probabilities are identified with the partition functions of a three-dimensional system.

•

The generating sums of the combinatorial weights in MLQs are readily identified with a simple trace of the
t
-oscillators.

•

The rank-reducing
RTT=TTR
relation for proving the ZF algebra is linked with the standard solution of the Yang–Baxter equation constructed from the symmetric tensor representations of the quantum group (see Remark
23
).

The second item above clarifies the origin of the tensor structure of

X_{\alpha}
questioned at the end of
[
ANP23
, Sec.4.2]
.
The conservation law in the strange five vertex model is designed
around the MLQ pairing, providing a natural framework for such analysis.
This paper presents these results concisely, without the need for heavy
machinery from integrable probability, making it both accessible and efficient to read.

The outline of the paper is as follows.
In Section
2
, we recall the
n
-ASEP and a general relation between
a matrix product formula
\tr(X_{\sigma_{1}}\cdots X_{\sigma_{L}})

for the stationary probabilities and the ZF algebra among the
spectral parameter-dependent operators

X_{0}(z),\ldots,X_{n}(z)
.

In Section
3
, we reexamine the combinatorial approach to stationary states
by the MLQ construction
[
CMW22
,
Mar20
]
.
We demonstrate that the result can be expressed
as a certain composition
\mathbb{M}(q,t)
of linear operators
which naturally lends itself to a diagrammatic representation as a CTM of size
n

(Proposition
9
and (
46
)).

In Section
4
, we introduce the strange five vertex model, whose
statistical weights take values in a
t
-oscillator algebra
\mathcal{A}
.
This model can be interpreted as a three dimensional (3D) system,
where
\mathcal{A}
acts on Fock spaces in the third dimension.
We establish that the stationary probabilities correspond to the partition function of this 3D system,
with boundary conditions derived from the
n
-ASEP configuration
(Theorem
13
and (
77
)).
The matrix product operators
X_{0}(z),\ldots,X_{n}(z)
play the role of
the layer transfer matrices of the system (
78
).
They obey a recursion relation with respect to the rank
n
,
which follows directly from the CTM diagrams.

In Section
5
, we provide a new proof of
the ZF algebra relation among
X_{0}(z),\ldots,X_{n}(z)
.
It is the most natural one from the perspective of quantum integrable systems,
elucidating a precise relationship (
97
) with the Yang–Baxter equation of the relevant quantum
R
matrices.

Section
6
is devoted to concluding remarks.

After the basic definitions of the model in Section
2
,
the text can also be read in the following order:
Section
4
and Section
5
,
to establish the matrix product formula first before proceeding
to Section
3
, where the connection with the MLQ method is explained.

2.
Multispecies ASEP

2.1.
Definition of
n
-ASEP

Consider the periodic 1D lattice with
L
sites, which will be denoted by
{\mathbb{Z}}_{L}
.
Each site
i\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{L}
is assigned with a variable

\sigma_{i}\in\{0,1,\ldots,n\}
, where

\sigma_{i}=\alpha
is interpreted that the site
i
is occupied by a particle of type
\alpha

if
\alpha\neq 0
and vacant if
\alpha=0
.
We assume
1\leq n<L
throughout.
The space of states is given by

(1)

\displaystyle(\mathbb{C}^{n})^{\otimes L}\simeq\bigoplus_{(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{L})\in\{0,\ldots,n\}^{L}}\mathbb{C}|\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{L}\rangle.

Consider a stochastic process
in which neighboring pairs of local states

(\sigma_{i},\sigma_{i+1})=(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})

are interchanged as
(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})\rightarrow(\sigma^{\prime},\sigma)

with the transition rate
t^{\theta(\sigma<\sigma^{\prime})}
with some parameter
t\geq 0
.
Here and in what follows we use the notation
\theta(\text{true})=1
and
\theta(\text{false})=0
.
Let
P(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{L};T)
be the probability of finding
the state
\lvert\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{L}\rangle
at time
T
, and set

(2)

\displaystyle|P(T)\rangle=\sum_{(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{L})\in\{0,\ldots,n\}^{L}}P(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{L};T)|\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{L}\rangle.

By
n
-ASEP we mean a Markov process
governed by the continuous-time master equation

(3)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dT}|P(T)\rangle=H|P(T)\rangle,

where the Markov matrix
1
1

1

Also called a “Hamiltonian” by abuse of terminology despite it not being Hermitian in general.

has the form

(4)

\displaystyle H

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{L}}H^{loc}_{i,i+1},\qquad H^{loc}\colon\lvert\alpha,\beta\rangle\mapsto(\lvert\beta,\alpha\rangle-\lvert\alpha,\beta\rangle)t^{\theta(\alpha<\beta)},

where
H^{loc}_{i,i+1}
acts on the
i
th and the
(i\!+\!1)
th components
as
H^{loc}
and as the identity elsewhere.
By the definition
H^{loc}
is expressed as

(5)

\displaystyle H^{loc}

\displaystyle=\sum_{0\leq\alpha<\beta\leq n}(tE_{\beta\alpha}\otimes E_{\alpha\beta}-tE_{\alpha\alpha}\otimes E_{\beta\beta}+E_{\alpha\beta}\otimes E_{\beta\alpha}-E_{\beta\beta}\otimes E_{\alpha\alpha})

in terms of the matrix unit
E_{\alpha\beta}
acting as

E_{\alpha\beta}\lvert\gamma\rangle=\delta_{\beta\gamma}\lvert\alpha\rangle
.

Example 1
.

Consider
n=1
and
L=3
, then we have

H^{loc}_{1,2}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
&0&0&0\\
&0&-t&1\\
&0&t&-1\\
&&&&-t&1&0\\
&&&&t&-1&0\\
&&&&0&0&0\\
&&&&&&&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{matrix}\lvert 000\rangle\\
\lvert 001\rangle\\
\lvert 010\rangle\\
\lvert 100\rangle\\
\lvert 011\rangle\\
\lvert 101\rangle\\
\lvert 110\rangle\\
\lvert 111\rangle\end{matrix},\quad H=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
&A&1&t\\
&t&A&1\\
&1&t&A\\
&&&&A&1&t\\
&&&&t&A&1\\
&&&&1&t&A\\
&&&&&&&0\end{pmatrix},

where
A=-t-1
and
H=H_{1,2}^{loc}+H_{2,3}^{loc}+H_{3,1}^{loc}
with noting
H_{3,1}^{loc}=H_{3,4}^{loc}
by convention.

As
H
preserves the particle content,
it acts on each
sector
labeled with
the
multiplicity

\mathbf{m}=(m_{0},\ldots,m_{n})\in({\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0})^{n+1}
of the particles:

(6)

\displaystyle W(\mathbf{m})=\!\!\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\in\Sigma(\mathbf{m})}\!\mathbb{C}\lvert{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\rangle,\quad\Sigma(\mathbf{m})=\{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}=(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{L})\in\{0,\ldots,n\}^{L}\;\mid\;\sum_{j=1}^{L}\delta_{\alpha,\sigma_{j}}=m_{\alpha},\forall\alpha\}.

Note that
m_{0}+\cdots+m_{n}=L
holds and
\dim W(\mathbf{m})=\frac{L!}{m_{0}!\cdots m_{n}!}
.
A sector
W(m_{0},\ldots,m_{n})
such that
m_{\alpha}\geq 1
for all
0\leq\alpha\leq n
is called
basic
.
Non-basic sectors are equivalent to a basic sector for
n^{\prime}
-ASEP with some
n^{\prime}<n
by a suitable relabeling of species.
Thus we shall exclusively deal with basic sectors in this paper
(hence
n\leq L
as mentioned before).

Example 2
.

Consider
n=2
and
L=4
.
Then the matrix
H
restricted to the sector
\mathbf{m}=(2,1,1)
is

\begin{pmatrix}A&1&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&1&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&t&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}\\
t&B&1&1&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&t\\
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&t&C&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&1&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&t&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&t&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&A&1&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&1&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&t&t&B&1&1&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}\\
t&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&t&C&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&1&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&t&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&A&1&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&1\\
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&t&t&B&1&1&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&t&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&t&C&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&1&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&1&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&t&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&A&1&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}\\
1&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&t&t&B&1\\
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&1&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&t&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.8,0.8}0}&t&C\end{pmatrix}\begin{matrix}\lvert 0012\rangle\\
\lvert 0102\rangle\\
\lvert 1002\rangle\\
\lvert 0120\rangle\\
\lvert 1020\rangle\\
\lvert 0021\rangle\\
\lvert 1200\rangle\\
\lvert 0201\rangle\\
\lvert 0210\rangle\\
\lvert 2001\rangle\\
\lvert 2010\rangle\\
\lvert 2100\rangle\end{matrix}

where
A=-2t-1
,
B=-2t-2
, and
C=-t-2
.
Note that the left null eigenvector of the Markov matrix
H
is
(1,\ldots,1)
reflecting the total probability conservation.

2.2.
Stationary states

In each sector
W(\mathbf{m})
there is a unique state

\lvert\overline{P}(\mathbf{m})\rangle
up to a normalization, called the
stationary state
,
satisfying
H\lvert\overline{P}(\mathbf{m})\rangle=0
.

The stationary state for
1
-TASEP is uniform in that all the configurations are realized with an equal probability.

Example 3
.

We present (unnormalized) steady states in small sectors of

2
-ASEP and
3
-ASEP in the form

\displaystyle\lvert\overline{P}(\mathbf{m})\rangle=\lvert\xi(\mathbf{m})\rangle+C\lvert\xi(\mathbf{m})\rangle+\cdots+C^{L-1}\lvert\xi(\mathbf{m})\rangle

where
C
denotes a cyclic shift

C\lvert\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{L}\rangle=\lvert\sigma_{L},\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{L-1}\rangle
.
Note that the choice of
\lvert\xi(\mathbf{m})\rangle
is not unique.

\displaystyle\lvert\xi(1,1,1)\rangle

\displaystyle=(2+t)\lvert 012\rangle+(1+2t)\lvert 021\rangle,

\displaystyle\lvert\xi(2,1,1)\rangle

\displaystyle=(3+t)\lvert 0012\rangle+2(1+t)\lvert 0102\rangle+(1+3t)\lvert 1002\rangle,

\displaystyle\lvert\xi(1,2,1)\rangle

\displaystyle=(2+t+t^{2})\lvert 0112\rangle+(1+t)^{2}\lvert 1012\rangle+(1+t+2t^{2})\lvert 1102\rangle,

\displaystyle\lvert\xi(1,1,2)\rangle

\displaystyle=(3+t)\lvert 1220\rangle+2(1+t)\lvert 2120\rangle+(1+3t)\lvert 2210\rangle,

\displaystyle\lvert\xi(1,2,2)\rangle

\displaystyle=(3+t+t^{2})\lvert 11220\rangle+(2+t+2t^{2})\lvert 12120\rangle+(1+3t+t^{2})\lvert 12210\rangle

\displaystyle+(2+t+2t^{2})\lvert 21120\rangle+(1+2t+2t^{2})\lvert 21210\rangle+(1+t+3t^{2})\lvert 22110\rangle,

\displaystyle\lvert\xi(2,1,2)\rangle

\displaystyle=(1+6t+7t^{2}+6t^{3})\lvert 00221\rangle+(2+7t+6t^{2}+5t^{3})\lvert 02021\rangle+(1+t)(3+4t+3t^{2})\lvert 02201\rangle

\displaystyle+(1+t)(3+4t+3t^{2})\lvert 20021\rangle+(5+6t+7t^{2}+2t^{3})\lvert 20201\rangle+(6+7t+6t^{2}+t^{3})\lvert 22001\rangle,

\displaystyle\lvert\xi(2,2,1)\rangle

\displaystyle=(3+t+t^{2})\lvert 00112\rangle+(2+2t+t^{2})\lvert 01012\rangle+(2+t+2t^{2})\lvert 01102\rangle

\displaystyle+(1+3t+t^{2})\lvert 10012\rangle+(1+2t+2t^{2})\lvert 10102\rangle+(1+t+3t^{2})\lvert 11002\rangle,

\displaystyle\lvert\xi(1,1,1,1)\rangle

\displaystyle=(9+7t+7t^{2}+t^{3})\lvert 0123\rangle+(3+11t+5t^{2}+5t^{3})\lvert 0213\rangle

\displaystyle+3(1+t)^{3}\lvert 1023\rangle+(5+5t+11t^{2}+3t^{3})\lvert 1203\rangle

\displaystyle+3(1+t)^{3}\lvert 2013\rangle+(1+7t+7t^{2}+9t^{3})\lvert 2103\rangle.

These formulas reduce to
[
KMO16
, Ex.2.1]
at
t=0
.
The result
\lvert\xi(1,1,1,1)\rangle
agrees with the anti-clockwise reading of
[
Mar20
, Fig.1.3]
with
q\rightarrow t
, if the local states
1,2,3,\cdot
therein are replaced by
3,2,1,0
here, respectively.
Moreover, according to
\lvert\xi(2,1,1)\rangle
in the above, the right null eigenvector of the Markov matrix
H
from Example
2
, the nontrivial stationary state up to normalization, is equal to the (column) vector

[3+t,2(1+t),1+3t,3+t,2(1+t),1+3t,3+t,2(1+t),1+3t,3+t,2(1+t),1+3t]^{T}.

2.3.
Matrix product construction

Consider the stationary state

(7)

\displaystyle\lvert\overline{P}(\mathbf{m})\rangle=\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\in\Sigma(\mathbf{m})}\mathbb{P}({\boldsymbol{\sigma}})\lvert{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\rangle

and suppose that the stationary probability
\mathbb{P}({\boldsymbol{\sigma}})

is expressed in the matrix product form

(8)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{L})=\tr(X_{\sigma_{1}}\cdots X_{\sigma_{L}})

in terms of some (not necessarily unique) operators
X_{0},\ldots,X_{n}
.
Introduce the notations for the matrix elements of the local Markov matrix (
5
) and
the associated product of
X_{i}
’s as

(9)

\displaystyle H^{loc}\lvert\alpha,\beta\rangle=\sum_{\gamma,\delta}h^{\gamma,\delta}_{\alpha,\beta}\lvert\gamma,\delta\rangle,\qquad(hXX)_{\alpha,\beta}:=\sum_{\gamma,\delta}h^{\alpha,\beta}_{\gamma,\delta}X_{\gamma}X_{\delta}.

Then we have

\displaystyle H\lvert\overline{P}(\mathbf{m})\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{L}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\Sigma(\mathbf{m})}\mathbb{P}(\ldots,\sigma_{i},\sigma_{i+1},\ldots)H^{loc}_{i,i+1}\lvert\ldots,\sigma_{i},\sigma_{i+1},\ldots\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{L}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\Sigma(\mathbf{m})}\sum_{\sigma^{\prime}_{i},\sigma^{\prime}_{i+1}}\tr(\cdots X_{\sigma_{i}}X_{\sigma_{i+1}}\cdots)h^{\sigma^{\prime}_{i},\sigma^{\prime}_{i+1}}_{\sigma_{i},\sigma_{i+1}}\lvert\ldots,\sigma^{\prime}_{i},\sigma^{\prime}_{i+1},\ldots\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\Sigma(\mathbf{m})}\sum_{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{L}}\tr(\cdots(hXX)_{\sigma_{i},\sigma_{i+1}}\cdots)\lvert\ldots,\sigma_{i},\sigma_{i+1},\ldots\rangle.

Therefore if there are another set of operators

{\widehat{X}}_{1},\ldots,{\widehat{X}}_{n}
obeying the
hat relation

(10)

\displaystyle(hXX)_{\alpha,\beta}=X_{\alpha}{\widehat{X}}_{\beta}-{\widehat{X}}_{\alpha}X_{\beta},

the vector (
7
) satisfies
H\lvert\overline{P}(\mathbf{m})\rangle=0

thanks to the cyclicity of the trace.
Then (
8
), assuming it is non-zero and finite, must coincide with the actual stationary probability
up to an overall normalization due to the uniqueness of the stationary state.
Note on the other hand that
{\widehat{X}}_{i}
satisfying the hat relation (
10
)
for a given
X_{i}
is not unique.
For example

{\widehat{X}}_{i}\rightarrow{\widehat{X}}_{i}+cX_{i}
keeps (
10
) valid.

From (
5
) we find the explicit form of (
10
) as

(11)

\displaystyle t^{\theta(\alpha>\beta)}X_{\beta}X_{\alpha}-t^{\theta(\alpha<\beta)}X_{\alpha}X_{\beta}=X_{\alpha}\widehat{X}_{\beta}-\widehat{X}_{\alpha}X_{\beta}\quad(0\leq\alpha,\beta\leq n).

It is easily seen that (
11
) is satisfied by setting

(12)

\displaystyle X_{\alpha}=X_{\alpha}(1),\qquad\widehat{X}_{\alpha}=(1-t)\left.\frac{dX_{\alpha}(z)}{dz}\right\rvert_{z=1}

for the operator

X_{0}(z),\ldots,X_{n}(z)
involving a spectral parameter
z

provided that they obey the relations

(13)

\displaystyle(x-ty)X_{\alpha}(y)X_{\beta}(x)

\displaystyle=(1-t)xX_{\alpha}(x)X_{\beta}(y)+(x-y)X_{\beta}(x)X_{\alpha}(y)\quad(0\leq\alpha<\beta\leq n),

(14)

\displaystyle[X_{\alpha}(x),X_{\beta}(y)]

\displaystyle=[X_{\alpha}(y),X_{\beta}(x)]\quad(0\leq\alpha,\beta\leq n).

The relation (
13
) allows one to interchange the order of
the spectral parameters
y,x
into
x,y
for
\alpha<\beta
.
An analogous relation for
\alpha>\beta
can be derived
by combining (
14
) and (
13
) as

\displaystyle X_{\alpha}(y)X_{\beta}(x)

\displaystyle=X_{\beta}(x)X_{\alpha}(y)-X_{\beta}(y)X_{\alpha}(x)+X_{\alpha}(x)X_{\beta}(y)

\displaystyle=\left(1-\frac{(1-t)x}{x-ty}\right)X_{\beta}(x)X_{\alpha}(y)+\left(1-\frac{x-y}{x-ty}\right)X_{\alpha}(x)X_{\beta}(y).

In this way, one finds that (
13
) and (
14
) are presented in the form of a

Zamolodchikov–Faddeev (ZF) algebra
:

(15)

\displaystyle X_{\alpha}(y)X_{\beta}(x)=\sum_{\gamma,\delta=0}^{n}R\bigl(y/x\bigr)^{\beta,\alpha}_{\gamma,\delta}X_{\gamma}(x)X_{\delta}(y).

Here the structure function is given by

(16)

\displaystyle R(z)^{\alpha,\alpha}_{\alpha,\alpha}=1,\qquad R(z)^{\alpha,\beta}_{\alpha,\beta}=\frac{(1-z)t^{\theta(\alpha<\beta)}}{1-tz},\qquad R(z)^{\beta,\alpha}_{\alpha,\beta}=\frac{(1-t)z^{\theta(\alpha>\beta)}}{1-tz},

for
\alpha\neq\beta
.
The other elements are zero.
This is known as a quantum
R
matrix for the vector representation of
U_{t}(\widehat{sl}_{n+1})

[
Dri87
,
Jim85
]
.
Set
\widecheck{R}(z)=PR(z)
with
P
being the transposition
P(u\otimes v)=v\otimes u
.
This satisfies the Yang–Baxter relation (cf.
[
Bax89
]
)

(17)

\displaystyle\widecheck{R}_{23}(y)\widecheck{R}_{12}(xy)\widecheck{R}_{23}(x)=\widecheck{R}_{12}(x)\widecheck{R}_{23}(xy)\widecheck{R}_{12}(y),

which is the associativity of (
15
).
The
R
matrix is stochastic in the sense that

\sum_{\alpha,\beta}R^{\alpha,\beta}_{\gamma,\delta}(z)=1
for any
0\leq\gamma,\delta\leq n
.

3.
Multiline queue construction

We recapitulate the multiline queue (MLQ) construction of ASEP states by
[
Mar20
]
in a form adapted to our conventions.
See also
[
CMW22
]
.
We also reformulate it as a composition of the matrix
\widecheck{M}(z,t)
, which we introduce as the building block of the construction.

3.1.
Ball system

We use the following notations:

(18)

\displaystyle\mathbf{i}=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{L})\in\{0,1\}^{L},\qquad\lvert\mathbf{i}\rvert=i_{1}+\cdots+i_{L},\qquad\mathbf{i}\leq\mathbf{j}\overset{\text{def}}{\Longleftrightarrow}\mathbf{j}-\mathbf{i}\in({\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0})^{L},

(19)

\displaystyle B_{l}=\{\mathbf{i}=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{L})\in\{0,1\}^{L}\mid\lvert\mathbf{i}\rvert=l\}.

Consider a basic sector
W(\mathbf{m})
for
\mathbf{m}=(m_{0},\ldots,m_{n})
, and set

(20)

\displaystyle l_{i}

\displaystyle=m_{i}+m_{i+1}+\cdots+m_{n}\quad(0\leq i\leq n),

(21)

\displaystyle B(\mathbf{m})

\displaystyle=B_{l_{n}}\otimes B_{l_{n-1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes B_{l_{1}}.

We prefer to use
\otimes
to denote the product of sets rather than
\times
as we are treating these sets as basis elements (in the sense of Kashiwara’s crystal bases
[
Kas90
]
).
Note that
L=l_{0}>l_{1}>\cdots>l_{n}\geq 1
since the sector
W(\mathbf{m})
is assumed to be basic.
Elements of
B(\mathbf{m})
will be referred to as a
ball system
.

Consider a ball system given as
\mathbf{b}=\mathbf{b}_{n}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{b}_{1}\in B(\mathbf{m})
, where

\mathbf{b}_{i}=(b_{i1},\ldots,b_{iL})\in B_{l_{i}}
.
We identify
\mathbf{b}
with a
ball diagram
, which is an
n\times L
rectangular tableau
in which the box at the
i
th row and the
j
th column contains a ball if
b_{ij}=1
and is empty if
b_{ij}=0
.
Here and in what follows, the rows (resp. columns) are numbered from the top
(resp. the left) of the diagram.
The
r
-th row corresponding to
\mathbf{b}_{r}
will simply be called Row
r
.
A ball is understood as carrying the information of its location in the tableau.

Example 4
.

Consider the
3
-ASEP on the length
L=9
lattice in the sector

W(\mathbf{m})
with
{\bf m}=(2,3,2,2)
. We have
(l_{1},l_{2},l_{3})=(7,4,2)
.
Consider a ball system

(22a)

\displaystyle\mathbf{b}=\mathbf{b}_{3}\otimes\mathbf{b}_{2}\otimes\mathbf{b}_{1}\in B_{2}\otimes B_{4}\otimes B_{7},

(22b)

\displaystyle\mathbf{b}_{3}=(001010000),\quad\mathbf{b}_{2}=(110100010),\quad\mathbf{b}_{1}=(011111101).

Its corresponding ball diagram looks as follows:

Row 1
Row 2
Row 3

3.2.
Multiline queue

Let us introduce a
pairing
of a ball system.
Given a ball system

\mathbf{b}=\mathbf{b}_{n}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{b}_{1}\in B(\mathbf{m})
,
we set
\mathbf{b}^{(n)}=\mathbf{b},\mathbf{m}^{(n)}=\mathbf{m}
and
construct a family of smaller ball systems

(23)

\displaystyle\mathbf{b}^{(r)}\in B({\bf m}^{(r)})=B_{l^{(r)}_{r}}\otimes\cdots\otimes B_{l^{(r)}_{1}}\qquad(l^{(r)}_{i}=m_{i}+m_{i+1}+\cdots+m_{r})

in the order
r=n-1,n-2,\ldots,1
.
The component
B_{l^{(r)}_{i}}
corresponds to the Row
i
of the ball system
\mathbf{b}^{(r)}
.
Note that
l^{(r)}_{r}=m_{r}
.
Let
d_{1},\ldots,d_{m_{r}}
be the balls of Row
r
in the ball diagram of (
23
) from left to right in this order.
We pair the ball
d_{\alpha}
to a ball
d^{\prime}_{\alpha}
in Row
(r-1)
in the order
\alpha=1,2,\ldots,m_{r}
.
The choice of
d^{\prime}_{\alpha}
is arbitrary except that if there is a yet unpaired ball just above
d_{\alpha}
,
that must be selected. (Such a case is called trivial pairing.)
The partners
d^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,d^{\prime}_{m_{r}}
are not necessarily aligned from left to right
in Row
(r-1)
.
Let
d^{\prime\prime}_{1},\ldots,d^{\prime\prime}_{m_{r}}
be these balls read from left to right.
They are paired to the balls in Row
(r-2)
in this order similarly.
Repeating this process we get a sequence of injections

(24)

\displaystyle\{\text{balls in Row $r$ of $\mathbf{b}^{(r)}$}\}\hookrightarrow\{\text{balls in Row $(r-1)$ of $\mathbf{b}^{(r)}$}\}\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow\{\text{balls in Row $1$ of $\mathbf{b}^{(r)}$}\}.

Denote the final image by
\mathbf{c}_{r}\in B_{m_{r}}
.
This procedure will be referred to as the
(n+1-r)
th round of the whole pairing.
After completion of it, we assign a
color

r
to all the balls captured in the round, and
eliminate them. The resulting ball system defines
\mathbf{b}^{(r-1)}
.
A pairing of
\mathbf{b}\in B(\mathbf{m})
is obtained by doing the round
1,2,\ldots,n
in this order,
where the last round
n
actually does not introduce an injection but only
assigns the color
1
to the remaining balls in the Row 1.

A pairing of a ball system
\mathbf{b}\in B(\mathbf{m})
may be regarded as a collection

\phi=(\phi_{1,2},\ldots,\phi_{n-1,n})
of injections

(25)

\displaystyle\phi_{s-1,s}\colon\{\text{balls in Row $s$ of $\mathbf{b}$}\}\hookrightarrow\{\text{balls in Row $(s-1)$ of $\mathbf{b}$}\}

satisfying a certain condition.
A ball system assigned with a pairing is called a
multiline queue
(MLQ) and
denoted by
Q=(\phi,\mathbf{b})
.
We identify it with a MLQ diagram, which is the ball diagram endowed with an arrow
d^{\prime}\leftarrow d
assigned to
each pair of balls
d,d^{\prime}
such that
d^{\prime}=\phi_{r-1,r}(d)
for some

2\leq r\leq n
.
The arrow starts from
d
and proceeds to the left, cyclically wrapping if necessary, until it
reaches
d^{\prime}
upstairs.
There are in general many MLQs
Q=(\phi,\mathbf{b})
for a given ball system
\mathbf{b}
.
The set of MLQs built in this way based on the set of ball systems
B(\mathbf{m})
in
(
21
) will be denoted by MLQ
(\mathbf{m})
.

Each ball
d
in
Q=(\phi,\mathbf{b})\in\text{MLQ}(\mathbf{m})
is
uniquely colored as
\mathcal{C}(d)\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
by the rule explained in the above.
By construction, the colors of balls in Row
r
range over
\{r,r+1,\ldots,n\}
.
In particular, there are exactly
m_{\alpha}
balls of color
\alpha
for
1\leq\alpha\leq n

in Row 1 of any MLQ from
\text{MLQ}(\mathbf{m})
.
We will always understand that balls in MLQs have been colored.

Example 5
.

The following is a MLQ for the ball system in Example
4
.
Non-trivial pairings are labeled as
p_{1},\ldots,p_{4}
for later convenience.

Row 1
Row 2
Row 3
3
3
p_{2}
p_{1}
3
3
2
2
p_{3}
p_{4}
3
3

3.3.
ASEP state
\lvert P_{\text{MLQ}}(\mathbf{m})_{q}\rangle

Here we construct an ASEP state
\lvert P_{\text{MLQ}}(\mathbf{m})_{q}\rangle\in W(\mathbf{m})

in three steps.
It is known as the MLQ construction of the stationary state
[
CMW22
,
Mar20
]
.
For ASEP, only the
q=1
case is necessary.
However, we explain a generalization including generic
q
that was introduced in
[
CMW22
]
for applications to Macdonald polynomials.
We shall focus on the construction process here.
The connection to the matrix product method in Section
2.3
and a proof that

\lvert P_{\text{MLQ}}(\mathbf{m})_{q=1}\rangle
is indeed the stationary state will be presented in later sections.

Step 1:

We define a map from MLQs to ASEP configurations (see (
6
) for the
definition of
\Sigma(\mathbf{m})
)

(26)

\displaystyle\pi\colon\text{MLQ}(\mathbf{m})\rightarrow\Sigma(\mathbf{m});\qquad Q\mapsto(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{L})

by stating that the image is the configuration of colored balls in Row 1 of
Q
, where
empty boxes are regarded as 0.
Concretely,
\sigma_{j}=\mathcal{C}(d)\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
if
d
is
the ball corresponding to
b_{1,j}=1
and

\sigma_{j}=0
if
b_{1,j}=0
.
For
Q
in Example
5
,

\mathbf{b}_{1}=(b_{1,1},\ldots,b_{1,8})

is given in Example
4
and
\pi(Q)=(0,2,1,3,2,3,1,0,1)
.

Step 2:

We assign a
weight

{\wt}_{q,t}
to a MLQ
Q=(\phi,\mathbf{b})
as

(27)

\displaystyle\wt_{q,t}(Q)=\prod_{p}\wt_{q,t}(p),

where the product is taken over all the pairs of balls
p=(d^{\prime}\leftarrow d)
in
Q

specified by
\phi
as
d^{\prime}=\phi_{r-1,r}(d)
for some
2\leq r\leq n
.
Let
\mathcal{B}_{c,r}
be the set of the balls in Row
r
and Row
(r\!-\!1)
having the color
c
.
There are
m_{c}
such balls in both rows and they are paired by
\phi_{r-1,r}
.
We determine the weights in the following order according to the pairing procedure explained in the previous subsection:

(28)

\begin{split}&\mathcal{B}_{n,n},\mathcal{B}_{n,n-1},\ldots,\mathcal{B}_{n,2},\\
&\mathcal{B}_{n-1,n-1},\ldots,\mathcal{B}_{n-1,2},\\
&\qquad\cdots\\
&\mathcal{B}_{2,2}.\end{split}

The
i
th row here corresponds to the
i
th round of the pairing.
Following the previous subsection,
we consider the balls in Row
r
from left to right within each
\mathcal{B}_{c,r}
.
2
2

2

The sum of the resulting weights (
33
) is actually independent of this order as shown in
[
CMW22
, Lem.2.1]
.

When seeking a pairing partner of
a ball
d
in Row
r
, the balls
d^{\prime}
in Row
(r-1)
that are not yet paired are called
free
.
As mentioned after (
25
), the pairing
p=(d^{\prime}\leftarrow d)
is depicted as an arrow
going from
d
to the left cyclically until it ends at
d^{\prime}
upstairs.
Suppose that
d
and
d^{\prime}
are in the
j
-th and the
j^{\prime}
-th columns of the
MLQ diagram from the left.
If
j^{\prime}=j
, it is a trivial pairing and we set
\wt_{q,t}(p)=1
.
Suppose
j^{\prime}\neq j
.
Set
\delta_{\text{wrap}}=1
if the arrow is wrapping, i.e.,
j<j^{\prime}
.
Otherwise we set
\delta_{\text{wrap}}=0
.
The free balls in Row
(r-1)
in the columns
j^{\prime}+1,j^{\prime}+2,\ldots,j-1

(indices regarded as elements in
{\mathbb{Z}}_{L}
here) are called
skipped
.
Now the weight is given as

(29)

\displaystyle\wt_{q,t}(p)=\dfrac{(1-t)t^{\#\text{skipped}}q^{(c-r+1)\delta_{\text{wrap}}}}{1-q^{c-r+1}t^{\#\text{free}}}=\begin{cases}\dfrac{(1-t)t^{\#\text{skipped}}}{1-q^{c-r+1}t^{\#\text{free}}}&\text{if $j^{\prime}<j$},\\[6.99997pt]
\dfrac{(1-t)t^{\#\text{skipped}}q^{c-r+1}}{1-q^{c-r+1}t^{\#\text{free}}}&\text{if $j<j^{\prime}$}.\end{cases}

In Example
5
, the weights of the non-trivial pairings are

(30)

\begin{split}&\wt_{q,t}(p_{1})=\frac{qt^{2}(1-t)}{1-qt^{4}},\;\;\wt_{q,t}(p_{2})=\frac{1-t}{1-qt^{3}},\;\;\wt_{q,t}(p_{3})=\frac{t(1-t)}{1-q^{2}t^{6}},\;\;\wt_{q,t}(p_{4})=\frac{qt^{2}(1-t)}{1-qt^{5}},\end{split}

and
\wt_{q,t}(Q)
is the product of them.

Step 3:

By using
\pi
(
26
) and the weight (
27
),
the state
\lvert P_{\text{MLQ}}(\mathbf{m})_{q}\rangle
is constructed as

(31)

\lvert P_{\text{MLQ}}(\mathbf{m})_{q}\rangle=\sum_{Q\in\text{MLQ}(\mathbf{m})}\wt_{q,t}(Q)\lvert\pi(Q)\rangle.

We note that when
q=1
, the weight
\wt_{q,t}(Q)
is invariant under (horizontal)
{\mathbb{Z}}_{L}
cyclic shifts of
Q
.
Therefore
\lvert P_{\text{MLQ}}(\mathbf{m})_{q=1}\rangle
is translationally invariant.

Example 6
.

Consider the
(L,n)=(4,2)
case.
The MLQs contributing to the states
|1012\rangle,|1021\rangle,|2011\rangle\in W(1,2,1)

and their weights are given as

Summing them at
q=1
yields

(32)

\displaystyle(1+t+t^{2})\lvert P_{\text{MLQ}}(1,2,1)_{q=1}\rangle=(1+t)^{2}\lvert 1012\rangle+(1+t+2t^{2})\lvert 1021\rangle+(2+t+t^{2})\lvert 2011\rangle+\cdots,

which agrees with
\lvert\xi(1,2,1)\rangle
in Example
3
in view of the cyclic
{\mathbb{Z}}_{L}
symmetry.

3.4.
Matrix
\widecheck{M}

We introduce a matrix
\widecheck{M}

which describes the interaction between neighboring rows in MLQs.
Consider a MLQ for
n=2
case with
(l_{1},l_{2})=(m,l)\,(l<m)
.
It contains two rows and have the form

Q=(\phi,{\bf i}\otimes{\bf j})
with
{\bf i}\otimes{\bf j}\in B_{l}\otimes B_{m}
.
Here
{\bf i}=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{L})\in B_{l}

means that there is
i_{k}(=0,1)
ball in the
k
th column from the left in the lower Row 2.
Similarly
{\bf j}=(j_{1},\ldots,j_{L})\in B_{m}
specifies the positions of balls in the upper Row 1.

For any

\mathbf{a}\otimes\mathbf{b}\in B_{l}\otimes B_{m-l}
and

{\bf i}\otimes{\bf j}\in B_{l}\otimes B_{m}
with
l<m
, define
a generating function of the weights by

(33)

M(q,t)^{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}}_{{\bf i},{\bf j}}=\delta^{\mathbf{a}+\mathbf{b}}_{\bf j}\sum_{\phi}\text{wt}_{q,t}(Q),

where
\delta^{\bf x}_{\bf y}=\theta({\bf x}={\bf y})
.
The sum is taken over the pairings

\phi
satisfying the condition

(34)

\displaystyle\phi(\{\text{balls in ${\bf i}$}\})=\{\text{balls in $\mathbf{a}$}\}.

To summarize, the indices
\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}
of
M(q,t)^{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}}_{{\bf i},{\bf j}}
have the following meaning,
where the last column is an interpretation in the language of the queuing processes in
[
Mar20
]
:

(35)

\begin{array}[]{c|l|l}\text{Indices}&\text{ball picture}&\text{queuing process}\\
\hline\cr B_{l}\ni\mathbf{i}&\text{balls in the lower row}&\text{arrival}\\
B_{m}\ni\mathbf{j}&\text{balls in the upper row}&\text{service}\\
B_{l}\ni\mathbf{a}&\text{paired balls in the upper row}&\text{departure}\\
B_{m-l}\ni\mathbf{b}&\text{unpaired balls }\mathbf{j}-\mathbf{a}\text{ in the upper row}&\text{unused service}\\
\end{array}

The constraint
\mathbf{a}+\mathbf{b}={\bf j}
in (
33
) is natural from the queuing process interpretation.
In general, there are numerous choices for
\phi
contributing to the sum (
33
), with a maximum of
l!
.

Example 7
.

Consider the two MLQs for
(l_{1},l_{2})=(\alpha+\beta+1,2)
as follows:

\cdots
\cdots
\cdots
\cdots
\cdots
\cdots
\overbrace{\hskip 55.0pt}^{\alpha}
\overbrace{\hskip 55.0pt}^{\beta}

\cdots
\cdots
\cdots
\cdots
\cdots
\cdots
\overbrace{\hskip 55.0pt}^{\alpha}
\overbrace{\hskip 55.0pt}^{\beta}

For the corresponding
\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b},{\bf i},{\bf j}
one has

(36)

\displaystyle M(q,t)^{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}}_{{\bf i},{\bf j}}=\frac{t^{\beta-1}(1-t)}{1-qt^{\alpha+\beta+1}}\frac{1-t}{1-qt^{\alpha+\beta}}+\frac{qt^{\alpha+\beta}(1-t)}{1-qt^{\alpha+\beta+1}}\frac{t^{\beta-1}(1-t)}{1-qt^{\alpha+\beta}}=\frac{t^{\beta-1}(1-t)^{2}(1+qt^{\alpha+\beta})}{(1-qt^{\alpha+\beta})(1-qt^{\alpha+\beta+1})}.

For
l\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
, let
V_{l}
be the vector space having a basis
\{v_{\mathbf{b}}\}
labeled by
B_{l}
from
19
:

(37)

\displaystyle V_{l}=\bigoplus_{\mathbf{b}\in B_{l}}\mathbb{C}(q,t)v_{\mathbf{b}}.

For
l<m
, we define a linear operator

\widecheck{M}(z,t)
depending on
t
and another variable
z
by

(38a)

\displaystyle\widecheck{M}(z,t)\colon V_{l}\otimes V_{m}

\displaystyle\rightarrow V_{m-l}\otimes V_{l}

(38b)

\displaystyle v_{\mathbf{i}}\otimes v_{\mathbf{j}}\;

\displaystyle\mapsto\sum_{\mathbf{a}\otimes\mathbf{b}\in B_{l}\otimes B_{m-l}}M(z,t)^{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}}\,v_{\mathbf{b}}\otimes v_{\mathbf{a}}\qquad(\mathbf{i}\otimes\mathbf{j}\in B_{l}\otimes B_{m}),

where the double sum in the RHS is actually the single one

\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in B_{l},\mathbf{a}\leq\mathbf{j}}M(z,t)^{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{j}-\mathbf{a}}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}}\,v_{\mathbf{j}-\mathbf{a}}\otimes v_{\mathbf{a}}

since
M(z,t)^{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}}=0
unless
\mathbf{b}=\mathbf{j}-\mathbf{a}
by (
33
).

Remark 8
.

One might think that by introducing
M^{\prime}(q,t)^{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}}_{{\bf i},{\bf j}}:=\delta^{\mathbf{a}+\mathbf{b}}_{{\bf i}+{\bf j}}\sum_{\phi}\text{wt}_{q,t}(Q)
, setting

\displaystyle\widecheck{M}^{\prime}(z,t)\colon V_{l}\otimes V_{m}

\displaystyle\rightarrow V_{m}\otimes V_{l}

\displaystyle v_{\mathbf{i}}\otimes v_{\mathbf{j}}

\displaystyle\mapsto\sum_{\mathbf{a}\otimes\mathbf{b}\in B_{l}\otimes B_{m}}M^{\prime}(z,t)^{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}}\,v_{\mathbf{b}}\otimes v_{\mathbf{a}}

is more natural rather than (
33
) and (
38
) since it possesses the standard “weight conservation”’ property common in quantum
R
matrices.
The reason we employ the strange
\widecheck{M}(z,t)
in (
38
)
is to make it fit with the queuing process interpretation in (
35
)
and will further be detailed in the next subsection.
We additionally note that the Yang–Baxter equation

\widecheck{M}^{\prime}(x,t)_{1,2}\widecheck{M}^{\prime}(xy,t)_{2,3}\widecheck{M}^{\prime}(y,t)_{1,2}=\widecheck{M}^{\prime}(y,t)_{2,3}\widecheck{M}^{\prime}(xy,t)_{1,2}\widecheck{M}^{\prime}(x,t)_{2,3}

is not valid for generic
x
and
y
.

3.5.
ASEP state
|P_{\text{MLQ}}({\bf m})_{q}\rangle
from
\widecheck{M}

Let us depict (
38b
) in a conventional diagram for vertex models (see,
e.g.
,
[
Bax89
]
):

(39)

\hbox to57.76pt{\vbox to64.81pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 27.68227pt\lower-33.37672pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\quad\longleftrightarrow\quad M(z,t)^{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}},

where we are taking
\mathbf{b}=\mathbf{j}-\mathbf{a}
.
The arrows here and in the rest of this section, as seen in (
45
)
and (
46
), correspond to the indices from
B_{l}
in (
19
).
In contrast, the arrows in the next section, except those in (
64
)–(
68
),
carry a single integer. In order to distinguish them,
we use thick arrows for the former and thin arrows for the latter.

In what follows
\widecheck{M}(z,t)
will simply be denoted by
\widecheck{M}(z)
as the parameter
t
is fixed everywhere.
Let
\widecheck{M}(z)_{j,j-1}
be the operator acting on the
(j,j\!-\!1)
th components

V_{s_{j}}\otimes V_{s_{j-1}}
in
V_{s_{n}}\otimes\cdots\otimes V_{s_{1}}
as
\widecheck{M}(z)
and as the identity elsewhere (
s_{\alpha}
’s are arbitrary positive integers).

A key role in our work is played by the operator

(40)

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}(q,t)

\displaystyle:V_{l_{n}}\otimes V_{l_{n-1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes V_{l_{1}}\rightarrow V_{m_{1}}\otimes V_{m_{2}}\otimes\cdots\otimes V_{m_{n}},

where
m_{i}
’s and
l_{i}
’s are related by (
20
).
It is given as a composition of
\frac{1}{2}n(n-1)

\widecheck{M}
’s as

(41)

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}(q,t)

\displaystyle=A_{n-1}A_{n-2}\cdots A_{1},

(42)

\displaystyle A_{j}

\displaystyle=\widecheck{M}(q^{n-j})_{j+1,j}\widecheck{M}(q^{n-j-1})_{j+2,j+1}\cdots\widecheck{M}(q)_{n,n-1}.

Explicitly, it reads

(43)

\begin{split}\mathbb{M}(q,t)=\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\widecheck{M}(q)_{n,n-1}&\\
\times\widecheck{M}(q^{2})_{n-1,n-2}\widecheck{M}(q)_{n,n-1}&\\
\cdots\qquad\\
\times\widecheck{M}(q^{n-1})_{2,1}\widecheck{M}(q^{n-2})_{3,2}\cdots\widecheck{M}(q)_{n,n-1},\end{split}

where the
r
th row from the bottom is
A_{r}
.
Let us illustrate the
n=3
case:

(44)

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}(q,t)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\widecheck{M}(q)_{3,2}}_{A_{2}}\,\underbrace{\widecheck{M}(q^{2})_{2,1}\widecheck{M}(q)_{3,2}}_{A_{1}}.

The matrix elements of
A_{1}
for the transition

v_{\mathbf{b}_{3}}\otimes v_{\mathbf{b}_{2}}\otimes v_{\mathbf{b}_{1}}\rightarrow v_{\mathbf{b}^{\prime}_{2}}\otimes v_{\mathbf{b}^{\prime}_{1}}\otimes v_{{\bf c}_{3}}

and those of
A_{2}
for

v_{\mathbf{b}^{\prime}_{2}}\otimes v_{\mathbf{b}^{\prime}_{1}}\otimes v_{{\bf c}_{3}}\rightarrow v_{{\bf c}_{1}}\otimes v_{{\bf c}_{2}}\otimes v_{{\bf c}_{3}}
are depicted as

(45)

Let us explain the meaning of these diagrams along with Example
5
.
The left diagram in (
45
) for
A_{1}

shows the first round of the pairing process corresponding to the red arrows in Example
5
.
One lets
\mathbf{b}_{3}
on Row 3 “penetrate” Row 2 and then Row 1, obtaining the image
{\bf c}_{3}

which specifies the location of color 3 balls at the top. See the definition of
{\bf c}_{r}
given after (
24
).
The elements
\mathbf{b}^{\prime}_{2}
and
\mathbf{b}^{\prime}_{1}
represent the free balls left intact in Row 2 and Row 1
in the first round, respectively.
In the second round of the pairing, one lets
\mathbf{b}^{\prime}_{2}
interact with
\mathbf{b}^{\prime}_{1}

as indicated by the blue arrows in Example
5
.
This is depicted in the right diagram in (
45
) for
A_{2}
,
where
{\bf c}_{2}
and
{\bf c}_{1}
correspond to the color 2 and 1 balls in Row 1, respectively.
In this way,
{\bf c}_{1},{\bf c}_{2},{\bf c}_{3}
give the final list of color 1, 2, and 3 balls in Row 1.
The weight
\text{wt}(Q)_{q,t}
of the MLQ is equal to the element of
\mathbb{M}(q,t)
for the transition

v_{\mathbf{b}_{3}}\otimes v_{\mathbf{b}_{2}}\otimes v_{\mathbf{b}_{1}}\rightarrow v_{{\bf c}_{1}}\otimes v_{{\bf c}_{2}}\otimes v_{{\bf c}_{3}}
.
Its diagram is obtained by combining the two in (
45
).
It results in a
single
diagram in the left of the following:

(46)

n=3
:
\mathbf{b}_{1}
\mathbf{c}_{1}
\mathbf{b}_{2}
\mathbf{c}_{2}
\mathbf{b}_{3}
\mathbf{c}_{3}
q
q
q^{2}

n=4
:
\mathbf{b}_{1}
\mathbf{c}_{1}
\mathbf{b}_{2}
\mathbf{c}_{2}
\mathbf{b}_{3}
\mathbf{c}_{3}

\mathbf{c}_{4}

q^{3}

Note that
v_{\mathbf{c}_{1}}\otimes v_{\mathbf{c}_{2}}\otimes v_{\mathbf{c}_{3}}\in V_{m_{1}}\otimes V_{m_{2}}\otimes V_{m_{3}}
from (
38a
) and (
20
).
The right one in (
46
) is the
n=4
case.
From (
46
), the general
n
case is clear.
The diagram for
\mathbb{M}(q,t)
has the form of the
corner transfer matrix
(CTM)
of the NW quadrant in
[
Bax89
, Fig. 13.2]
.
3
3

3

It coincides with a wiring diagram of the longest element
of the symmetric group
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
.

The operator
A_{j}
(
42
) corresponds to the
j
th row from the bottom in the diagram.
It encodes the weights in the
j
th round of the pairing process, which concerns the balls in the
j
th line of (
28
) from the top, and they are colored in
n+1-j
.
The reason we employ the unusual
\mathbf{b}=\mathbf{j}-\mathbf{a}
weight conservation in (
38b
) and (
39
)
is that it is necessary to describe the unpaired balls, which remain active and will be the relevant players in the
subsequent rounds. See (
35
).

The outputs of
\mathbb{M}(q,t)
are superpositions of data of the form
v_{\mathbf{c}_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{\mathbf{c}_{n}}\in V_{m_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes V_{m_{n}}
.
They are transformed to the ASEP states in
W(\mathbf{m})
(
6
) by a simple “projection”:

(47)

\displaystyle\Pi\colon V_{m_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes V_{m_{n}}

\displaystyle\rightarrow W(\mathbf{m})

\displaystyle v_{\mathbf{c}_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{\mathbf{c}_{n}}

\displaystyle\mapsto\lvert\mathbf{c}_{1}+2\mathbf{c}_{2}+\cdots+n\mathbf{c}_{n}\rangle,

where the
0
th component of
\mathbf{m}=(m_{0},m_{1},\ldots,m_{n})
is determined by the condition
\lvert\mathbf{m}\rvert=L
.
To summarize the argument so far, we have explained that the state

\lvert P_{\text{MLQ}}(\mathbf{m})_{q}\rangle
constructed from the
MLQ approach is expressed as follows.

Proposition 9

(CTM interpretation of the MLQ construction)
.

(48)

\displaystyle\lvert P_{\text{MLQ}}(\mathbf{m})_{q}\rangle=\Pi\Bigl(\mathbb{M}(q,t)\sum_{\mathbf{b}_{n}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{b}_{1}\in B(\mathbf{m})}v_{\mathbf{b}_{n}}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{\mathbf{b}_{1}}\Bigr).

It is known to yield the actual stationary states at
q=1

[
Mar20
]
.

4.
t
-oscillator weighted five vertex model

We will compute the stationary probabilities by introducing a five vertex model.
It is different from those considered in
[
KMO15
,
KMO16
]
and
does not satisfy the usual weight conservation property.
Instead, it uses the same strange weight conservation as in the previous section.

4.1.
t
-deformed quantum oscillator

Our five vertex model will be weighted using a
t
-oscillator algebra
\mathcal{A}

generated by
\langle\mathbf{a}^{+},\mathbf{a}^{-},\mathbf{k}\rangle
satisfying the relations

(49)

\displaystyle\mathbf{k}\,\mathbf{a}^{\pm}=t^{\pm 1}\mathbf{a}^{\pm}\mathbf{k},\qquad\mathbf{a}^{-}\mathbf{a}^{+}=1-t\mathbf{k},\qquad\mathbf{a}^{+}\mathbf{a}^{-}=1-\mathbf{k}.

It has a natural representation from its triangular decomposition, which we refer to as the
bosonic Fock space
:

(50)

\displaystyle F:=\bigoplus_{d=0}^{\infty}{\mathbb{Q}}(t)\lvert d\rangle;\qquad\mathbf{k}\lvert d\rangle=t^{d}\lvert d\rangle,\quad\mathbf{a}^{+}\lvert d\rangle=\lvert d+1\rangle,\quad\mathbf{a}^{-}\lvert d\rangle=(1-t^{d})\lvert d-1\rangle,

extended by linearity with the convention
\lvert-1\rangle:=0
.
We will also use the “number” operator
\bf{h}
defined by

(51)

\mathbf{h}|d\rangle=d|d\rangle

so that
{\bf k}=t^{\mathbf{h}}
.

Remark 10
.

Consider a
\tau
-twist quantum oscillator algebra

\mathcal{A}_{\tau}
having the relations

(52)

\displaystyle\mathbf{k}\,\mathbf{a}^{\pm}=t^{\pm 1}\mathbf{a}^{\pm}\mathbf{k},\qquad\mathbf{a}^{-}\mathbf{a}^{+}=1-t^{\tau}\mathbf{k}^{\tau},\qquad\mathbf{a}^{+}\mathbf{a}^{-}=1-\mathbf{k}^{\tau}

and the representation of
F
:

(53)

\displaystyle\mathbf{k}\lvert d\rangle=t^{d}\lvert d\rangle,\qquad\mathbf{a}^{+}\lvert d\rangle=\lvert d+1\rangle,\qquad\mathbf{a}^{-}\lvert d\rangle=(1-t^{\tau d})\lvert d-1\rangle,

in place of (
49
) and (
50
).
In this paper, we will use
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}_{\tau=1}
only, but let us make a few remarks about the general case.
The representations of the quantum oscillator algebra can be used
to construct representations of a (Drinfel’d–Jimbo) quantum group (see,
e.g.
,
[
Hay90
,
Kun22
]
),
where the twist parameter
\tau
is related to the deformation parameter in parallel to
[
AS24
]
.
When
\tau=2
, this is the quantum oscillator algebra that has appeared in relation to the tetrahedron equation; see,
e.g.
,
[
KMO16
,
Kun22
]
.
The idea of realizing Lie algebra generators using bosons, i.e.,
creation and annihilation operators, in physics
dates back to
[
HP40
]
, which is referred to as the Holstein–Primakoff transformation.

t
-oscillator weighted five vertex model

S_{ij}^{ab}
as follows:

S_{ij}^{ab}\hfil\hskip 20.&1\hfil\hskip 20.&1\hfil\hskip 20.&\mathbf{k}\hfil\hskip 20.&\mathbf{a}^{-}\hfil\hskip 20.&\mathbf{a}^{+}\end{array}

We set
S_{ij}^{ab}=0
for all the other configurations.
Note that
{\bf k}
corresponds to the unique configuration such that
j-a>0
.
The weight conservation holds in an unusual form:

(55)

\displaystyle S^{ab}_{ij}=0\;\;\text{unless}\;\;\;a+b=j.

Compare (
55
) with (
33
).
We let the number operator
{\bf h}
also act on the
\{0,1\}
states.
Then (
54
) satisfies

(56)

z^{{\bf h}+a}S^{ab}_{ij}=S^{ab}_{ij}z^{{\bf h}+i},

which implies that the total weight of the first and the
t
-oscillator components is conserved.

Remark 11
.

The multispecies totally asymmetric simple exclusion process (TASEP) is the special case
corresponding to
t=0
in the ASEP.
The five vertex model utilized in
[
KMO15
, Eq.(2.20)]

for TASEP has the weights
\widetilde{S}^{ab}_{ij}|_{t=0}
,
where
\widetilde{S}^{ab}_{ij}
is given by

(57)

\begin{array}[]{c*{5}{@{\hspace{20pt}}c}}\hbox to42.38pt{\vbox to49.13pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 20.26978pt\lower-25.36432pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\widetilde{S}_{ij}^{ab}\hfil\hskip 20.&1\hfil\hskip 20.&1\hfil\hskip 20.&\mathbf{k}\hfil\hskip 20.&\mathbf{a}^{-}\hfil\hskip 20.&\mathbf{a}^{+}\end{array}

It satisfies the usual weight conservation, i.e.,
\widetilde{S}_{ij}^{ab}=0
unless
a+b=i+j
.
The two models are related by
S^{ab}_{ij}=\widetilde{S}^{a,b+i}_{ij}
.
Both can be interpreted as three-dimensional vertex models in which the
t
-oscillator acts in the third direction.
The model (
57
) works efficiently for TASEP
revealing the crystal theoretical nature
[
KMO15
]
of the MLQ method
[
FM07
]

and reducing the relevant ZF algebra to the tetrahedron equation at
t=0

[
KMO16
,
Kun22
]
.
Although the ZF algebras for TASEP and ASEP are smoothly connected via the parameter
t
,
we have not found a method to formulate the results in the present paper based on the model
(
57
).

Next, for any

\mathbf{a}\otimes\mathbf{b}\in B_{l}\otimes B_{m-l}
and

{\bf i}\otimes{\bf j}\in B_{l}\otimes B_{m}
with
l<m
, we define

S(q,t)^{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}}

by a matrix product formula

(58)

S(q,t)_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}}^{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}}=(1-qt^{m-\ell})\tr_{F}\bigl(q^{\mathbf{h}}S_{i_{1}j_{1}}^{a_{1}b_{1}}S_{i_{2}j_{2}}^{a_{2}b_{2}}\cdots S_{i_{L}j_{L}}^{a_{L}b_{L}}\bigr),

where the trace is taken over
F
.
When the trace space is clear, we will omit it from the notation.
From (
55
) it follows that

(59)

\displaystyle S(q,t)_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}}^{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}}=0\;\;\text{ unless}\;\;\mathbf{a}+\mathbf{b}=\mathbf{j}.

Moreover from
|\mathbf{j}|-|\mathbf{a}|=m-l>0
and the comment after (
54
),
there is at least one
{\bf k}
in the operators

S_{i_{1}j_{1}}^{a_{1}b_{1}},\ldots,S_{i_{L}j_{L}}^{a_{L}b_{L}}
.
Therefore the trace (
58
) is a valid formal power series in
t
even at
q=1
.
The definition (
58
) is depicted as
[
Kun22
, Fig.11.3]
|_{z\rightarrow q}

if the vertices therein are regarded as
S^{a_{r}b_{r}}_{i_{r}j_{r}}
.
See also (
75
) below.

Example 12
.

Suppose
(l,m)=(2,\alpha+\beta+1)
with
\alpha,\beta\geq 1
and take

\mathbf{a},{\bf i}\in B_{l},{\bf j}\in B_{m}
and
\mathbf{b}\in B_{m-l}
as

\mathbf{a}=(1,0^{\beta-1},0,1,0,0^{\alpha}),\;\mathbf{b}=(0,1^{\beta-1},0,0,0,1^{\alpha}),\;\mathbf{i}=(0,0^{\beta-1},1,0,1,0^{\alpha}),\;\mathbf{j}=(1,1^{\beta-1},0,1,0,1^{\alpha}),

which satisfies
\mathbf{a}+\mathbf{b}={\bf j}
.
Now (
58
) is calculated as

\displaystyle S(q,t)_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}}^{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}}

\displaystyle=(1-qt^{\alpha+\beta-1})\tr\bigl(q^{\mathbf{h}}S^{10}_{01}(S^{01}_{01})^{\beta-1}S^{00}_{10}S^{10}_{01}S^{00}_{10}(S^{01}_{01})^{\alpha}\bigr)

\displaystyle=(1-qt^{\alpha+\beta-1})\tr\bigl(q^{\mathbf{h}}\mathbf{a}^{-}\mathbf{k}^{\beta-1}\mathbf{a}^{+}\mathbf{a}^{-}\mathbf{a}^{+}\mathbf{k}^{\alpha}\bigr)

\displaystyle=(1-qt^{\alpha+\beta-1})\sum_{d\geq 0}q^{d}(1-t^{d+1})t^{(\beta-1)(d+1)}(1-t^{d+1})t^{\alpha d}=\frac{t^{\beta-1}(1-t)^{2}(1+qt^{\alpha+\beta})}{(1-qt^{\alpha+\beta})(1-qt^{\alpha+\beta+1})}.

Note that this coincides with
M(q,t)_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}}^{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}}
in (
36
).
The equality holds in general as shown in Theorem
13
below, which is
an essential result quantifying the algorithmic MLQ construction into the
t
-oscillator algebra.

Theorem 13
.

For any
\mathbf{a},\mathbf{i}\in B_{l},\mathbf{i}\in B_{m}
and
\mathbf{j}\in B_{m-l}
with
l<m
, the following equality is valid:

(60)

M(q,t)_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}}^{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}}=S(q,t)_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}}^{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}}.

Proof.

In view of (
33
) and (
59
), we shall exclusively consider the non-trivial situation
\mathbf{a}+\mathbf{b}=\mathbf{j}
.
We prove (
60
) by comparing the recursion relations with respect to
l
on both hand sides.
Let us illustrate the derivation for
S(q,t)_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}}^{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}}
using the example

\mathbf{i}=(000000110100),\mathbf{a}=(100010000100)\in B_{l=3}
,

\mathbf{j}=(100110001101)\in B_{m=6}
with
\mathbf{b}=\mathbf{j}-\mathbf{a}
and
L=12
.
According to (
35
), the ball diagram relevant to

M(q,t)_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}}^{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}}
looks as

(61)

\mathbf{a}^{-}
\mathbf{k}
\mathbf{a}^{-}
\mathbf{a}^{+}
\mathbf{a}^{+}
\mathbf{k}
1
\mathbf{k}

where the upper and lower rows stand for
\mathbf{j}
and
\mathbf{i}
, respectively, and

\mathbf{a}
is depicted as the red shaded balls.
4
4

4

This red shading has nothing to do with the color
\mathcal{C}
of balls
assigned in the MLQs explained in Section
3.2
.

We have also presented
S^{a_{1}b_{1}}_{i_{1}j_{1}},\ldots,S^{a_{L}b_{L}}_{i_{L}j_{L}}
that are not 1 at the bottom,
except for the third column from the right, for later convenience.
Thus (
58
) reads

\displaystyle S(q,t)_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}}^{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}}=(1-qt^{3})\Theta_{0},\qquad\Theta_{0}=\tr(q^{\mathbf{h}}\mathbf{a}^{-}\mathbf{k}\,\mathbf{a}^{-}\mathbf{a}^{+}\mathbf{a}^{+}\mathbf{k}\,1\mathbf{k}).

What we do is to move the leftmost
\mathbf{a}^{+}
here to the left
cyclically to go around the trace once by using the
following form of the relations (
49
) for
\mathcal{A}
:

(62a)

\displaystyle\mathbf{a}^{-}\mathbf{a}^{+}

\displaystyle=t\mathbf{a}^{+}\mathbf{a}^{-}+(1-t),

(62b)

\displaystyle\mathbf{k}\,\mathbf{a}^{+}

\displaystyle=t\mathbf{a}^{+}\mathbf{k},

(62c)

\displaystyle q^{\mathbf{h}}\mathbf{a}^{+}

\displaystyle=q\,\mathbf{a}^{+}q^{\mathbf{h}}.

By applying (
62a
), the trace
\Theta_{0}
is decomposed as

(63)

\Theta_{0}=\Theta^{\prime}_{0}+\Theta_{1},\qquad\Theta^{\prime}_{0}=(1-t)\tr(q^{\mathbf{h}}\mathbf{a}^{-}\mathbf{k}\,\mathbf{a}^{+}\mathbf{k}\,1\mathbf{k}),\qquad\Theta_{1}=t\tr(q^{\mathbf{h}}\mathbf{a}^{-}\mathbf{k}\,\mathbf{a}^{+}\mathbf{a}^{-}\mathbf{a}^{+}\mathbf{k}\,1\mathbf{k}).

The term
\Theta^{\prime}_{0}
corresponds to the following diagram where the connected balls should be understood absent:

(64)

1\!-\!t
\mathbf{a}^{-}
\mathbf{k}
\mathbf{a}^{+}
\mathbf{k}
1
\mathbf{k}

The trace

\Theta_{1}
is decomposed as

(65)

\begin{split}\Theta_{1}&=t^{2}\tr(q^{\mathbf{h}}\mathbf{a}^{-}\mathbf{a}^{+}\mathbf{k}\,\mathbf{a}^{-}\mathbf{a}^{+}\mathbf{k}\,1\mathbf{k})=\Theta^{\prime}_{1}+\Theta_{2},\\
\Theta^{\prime}_{1}&=t^{2}(1-t)\tr(q^{\mathbf{h}}\mathbf{k}\,\mathbf{a}^{-}\mathbf{a}^{+}\mathbf{k}\,1\mathbf{k}),\quad\Theta_{2}=t^{3}\tr(q^{\mathbf{h}}\mathbf{a}^{+}\mathbf{a}^{-}\mathbf{k}\,\mathbf{a}^{-}\mathbf{a}^{+}\mathbf{k}\,1\mathbf{k}).\end{split}

The term
\Theta^{\prime}_{1}
corresponds to the following diagram where the connected balls should be understood absent:

(66)

1\!-\!t
t
t
\mathbf{k}
\mathbf{a}^{-}
\mathbf{a}^{+}
\mathbf{k}
1
\mathbf{k}

The trace
\Theta_{2}
is decomposed by rewriting the operator
1
as
(1-t)+t
as

(67)

\begin{split}\Theta_{2}&=qt^{4}\tr(q^{\mathbf{h}}\mathbf{a}^{-}\mathbf{k}\,\mathbf{a}^{-}\mathbf{a}^{+}\mathbf{k}\,1{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!+}\mathbf{k})=\Theta^{\prime}_{2}+\Theta_{3},\\
\Theta^{\prime}_{2}&=qt^{4}(1-t)\tr(q^{\mathbf{h}}\mathbf{a}^{-}\mathbf{k}\,\mathbf{a}^{-}\mathbf{a}^{+}\mathbf{k}\,\mathbf{a}^{+}\mathbf{k}),\quad\Theta_{3}=qt^{6}\tr(q^{\mathbf{h}}\mathbf{a}^{-}\mathbf{k}\,\mathbf{a}^{-}\mathbf{a}^{+}\mathbf{a}^{+}\mathbf{k}\,\mathbf{k})=qt^{6}\Theta_{0}.\end{split}

The term
\Theta^{\prime}_{2}
corresponds to the following diagram where the connected balls should be understood absent:

(68)

q
t
t
t
1\!-\!t
t
\mathbf{a}^{-}
\mathbf{k}
\mathbf{a}^{-}
\mathbf{a}^{+}
\mathbf{k}
\mathbf{a}^{+}
\mathbf{k}

Thus we have the relation

(1-qt^{6})\tr(q^{\mathbf{h}}\mathbf{a}^{-}\mathbf{k}\,\mathbf{a}^{-}\mathbf{a}^{+}\mathbf{a}^{+}\mathbf{k}\,\mathbf{k})=(1-qt^{6})\Theta_{0}=\Theta^{\prime}_{0}+\Theta^{\prime}_{1}+\Theta^{\prime}_{2}.

The traces in
\Theta^{\prime}_{\alpha}
involve operators obtained by eliminating

{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!+}{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!-}
, with (
62a
) replaced by
1-t
if the target of the pairing
is free from a ball directly below it. If the target has a ball underneath,
the corresponding operator
1
is replaced by
{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!+}
.
The coefficient
t
(resp.
q
) in the RHS of (
62
) counts the number of skipped balls
(resp. wrapping).
In general an analogous manipulation yields

(69)

\displaystyle(1-qt^{m})\tr\bigl(q^{\mathbf{h}}S_{i_{1}j_{1}}^{a_{1}b_{1}}S_{i_{2}j_{2}}^{a_{2}b_{2}}\cdots S_{i_{L}j_{L}}^{a_{L}b_{L}}\bigr)=\sum_{\text{leftmost pairings}}\!\!(1-t)q^{\#\text{wrapped}}t^{\#\text{skipped}}\tr(\text{unpaired parts}),

where the sum extends over the leftmost pairings, which means those between the leftmost
\mathbf{a}^{+}
in the downstairs and
the red shaded balls, i.e.,
\mathbf{a}\in B_{l}
, in the upstairs. Thus there are
\lvert\mathbf{a}\rvert=l
summands.
Let
\mathbf{a}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime}\in B_{l-1},\mathbf{j}^{\prime}\in B_{m-1}
and
\mathbf{j}^{\prime}\in B_{(m-1)-(l-1)}=B_{m-l}

be the arrays corresponding to the unpaired parts.
Then from the definition (
58
), the recursion relation (
69
)
is translated as

(70)

\displaystyle S(q,t)^{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}}=\frac{1-t}{1-qt^{m}}\sum_{\text{leftmost pairings}}q^{\#\text{wrapped}}t^{\#\text{skipped}}S(q,t)^{\mathbf{a}^{\prime},\mathbf{b}^{\prime}}_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime},\mathbf{j}^{\prime}}.

On the other hand, from the queuing algorithm explained in the previous section,
it is clear that the same recursion relation holds also for
M(q,t)^{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}}
.
In particular, the factor
(1-qt^{m})^{-1}
emerges from the fact that
the denominators
1-qt^{\#\text{free}}
in (
29
) lead to

\prod_{r=1}^{l}(1-qt^{m+1-r})
for
M(q,t)^{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}}

whereas
\prod_{r=1}^{l-1}(1-qt^{m-r})
for
M(q,t)^{\mathbf{a}^{\prime},\mathbf{b}^{\prime}}_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime},\mathbf{j}^{\prime}}
.
It follows, by induction on
l
, the proof of (
60
) reduces formally to
l=0
.
When
l=0
, one has
M(q,t)^{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}}=\delta^{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathbf{j}}
from (
33
),
and
S(q,t)^{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}}=(1-qt^{m})\tr(q^{\mathbf{h}}\mathbf{k}^{m})\delta^{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathbf{j}}=\delta^{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathbf{j}}
from
(
54
) and (
58
).
This completes the proof.
∎

Recall from (
33
) that
M(q,t)^{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}}
consists of
numerous summands,
which have to be calculated resorting to pairing diagrams by counting the skipped/free balls and wrapping.
Theorem
13
identifies it with a single trace that is free from such pairing details.
Put in the other way,
M(q,t)^{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}}
was giving a combinatorial description for an expansion
of the trace
S(q,t)^{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}}
.

In view of Theorem
13
, the interpretation of the multi-indices
\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b},{\bf i},{\bf j}
in (
35
)
and the layer structure in (
77
), the five vertex weights in (
54
) describes
a
local
event in the queuing process with respect to the “time”
\alpha\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{L}
,
where the number of the custmors in the queue is increased or decreased by
{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!+}_{\alpha}
or
{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!-}_{\alpha}
.

4.3.
Operator
\mathbb{S}(q,t)

A key ingredient in Proposition
9
is the operator
\mathbb{M}(q,t)
whose
building block was
\widecheck{M}(q,t)
in (
38
).
From Theorem
13
, it is natural to reformulate them in terms of

S(q,t)^{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}}
.
This is what we do in this subsection as a preparation for the introduction of the operator
X_{\alpha}(z)

in the next subsection.

For
l<m
, define a linear operator
\widecheck{S}(q,t)
in parallel with
\widecheck{M}(q,t)
in (
38
):

(71a)

\displaystyle\widecheck{S}(q,t)\colon V_{l}\otimes V_{m}

\displaystyle\rightarrow V_{m-l}\otimes V_{l}

(71b)

\displaystyle v_{\mathbf{i}}\otimes v_{\mathbf{j}}\;

\displaystyle\mapsto\sum_{\mathbf{a}\otimes\mathbf{b}\in B_{l}\otimes B_{m-l}}S(q,t)^{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}}\,v_{\mathbf{b}}\otimes v_{\mathbf{a}}=\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in B_{l},\mathbf{a}\leq\mathbf{j}}S(q,t)^{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{j}-\mathbf{a}}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}}\,v_{\mathbf{j}-\mathbf{a}}\otimes v_{\mathbf{a}}\qquad(\mathbf{i}\otimes\mathbf{j}\in B_{l}\otimes B_{m}),

where the last equality in (
71b
) is due to (
59
).
By Theorem
13
we know

(72)

\displaystyle\widecheck{M}(q,t)=\widecheck{S}(q,t).

Substituting this into (
43
) we have

(73)

\begin{split}\mathbb{M}(q,t)=\mathbb{S}(q,t):=\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\widecheck{S}(q)_{n,n-1}&\\
\times\widecheck{S}(q^{2})_{n-1,n-2}\widecheck{S}(q)_{n,n-1}&\\
\cdots\qquad\\
\times\widecheck{S}(q^{n-1})_{2,1}\widecheck{S}(q^{n-2})_{3,2}\cdots\widecheck{S}(q)_{n,n-1}.\end{split}

From these definitions, Proposition
9
can be restated as

Corollary 14
.

(74)

\displaystyle\lvert P_{\text{MLQ}}(\mathbf{m})_{q}\rangle=\Pi\Bigl(\mathbb{S}(q,t)\sum_{\mathbf{b}_{n}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{b}_{1}\in B(\mathbf{m})}v_{\mathbf{b}_{n}}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{\mathbf{b}_{1}}\Bigr).

4.4.
Matrix product operators
X_{\alpha}(z)

In the remainder of this section we concentrate on the
q=1
case, which is relevant to the actual stationary states on the
n
-ASEP.
We do not exhibit
z
in the vertex diagram (
39
) and
q
in the diagrams like (
45
) and (
46
)
assuming that they are all set to
1
.
We depict (
58
) as

(75)

S^{a_{r}b_{r}}_{i_{r}j_{r}}
taking values in the
t
-oscillator algebra as in (
54
).
Blue arrows are added to signify the Fock space
F
(
50
) on which it acts and the trace is taken.
The operator
\widecheck{S}(1,t)
from (
71
) will similarly be depicted by suppressing the indices.

The operator
\mathbb{S}(1,t)
shares the same corner transfer matrix (CTM) diagram representation
as
\mathbb{M}(1,t)
in (
46
), with each vertex (
39
) of thick arrows
now possessing the structure in the “third dimension” as illustrated in (
75
).
As the result, the stationary state in Corollary
14
is given as

(76)

\displaystyle\lvert P_{\text{MLQ}}(\mathbf{m})_{q=1}\rangle=\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\in\Sigma(\mathbf{m})}\mathbb{P}({\boldsymbol{\sigma}})\lvert{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\rangle,

where the (unnormalized) stationary probability
\mathbb{P}({\boldsymbol{\sigma}})
, for example for
n=3

where there are
L
layers of the CTM.

The dependence on
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}=(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{L})

is reflected in the boundary condition.
In fact, comparison of (
77
) and (
8
) amounts to identifying the

i
th layer with
X_{\sigma_{i}}
.
Moreover, inspecting the projection
\Pi
in (
47
) leads to the constraint that

X_{\alpha}\,(\alpha=0,\ldots,n)
is a partition function of the five vertex model
in the NW quadrant
with a free boundary condition at the bottom and a fixed one on the right,
where the edge states
s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}
on the outgoing arrows (numbered from the top)
are specified as
s_{i}=\delta_{i,\alpha}
.
Such a CTM diagram representation of the stationary probabilities was first obtained for TASEP
(the
t=0
case) in
[
KMO15
,
KMO16
]
using the five vertex model in Remark
11
.
See the recent work
[
IMO24
]
for a new application.

Recall from Section
2.3
that one also needs the spectral parameter dependent version

X_{\alpha}(z)
of
X_{\alpha}
in order to establish the stationary condition
by resorting to the ZF algebra (
15
) independently from the MLQ construction.

Definition 15

(The operator
X_{\alpha}(z)
)
.

We introduce the operator
X_{\alpha}(z)\,(\alpha=0,\ldots,n)

as the CTM with each vertex having a
t
-oscillator valued weight specified in (
54

where the
z^{\mathbf{h}}
are denoted by
\bullet
.
The diagram indicates that all edge variables are summed over
\{0,1\}
.
The variables on the right outgoing edges are fixed as shown,
while there is no boundary condition along the bottom.
This depiction should be understood as having an additional arrow
at each vertex, perpendicular to the layer, corresponding to the

t
-oscillator acting on its own Fock space.

.

n=2\hfil\hskip 50.&n=3\end{array}

Obviously this
X_{\alpha}(z)
reduces to the
X_{\alpha}
considered previously when
z=1
.
It is an element of
\mathrm{End}(F^{\otimes\frac{n(n-1)}{2}})
.
We number the vertices in (
78
)
from top to bottom in the rightmost column, and then similarly in the second rightmost column, and so on, as

1,\ldots,\frac{n(n-1)}{2}
in this order.
The
t
-oscillator generators acting on the
i
th copy of the Fock space
F
in this numbering
will be distinguished by the subscript
i
when
n\geq 3
.
Thus, the generators with different subscripts commute.
To summarize, we have proved the following.

Theorem 17

(Five vertex CTM interpretation of the MLQ construction)
.

The MLQ construction in Section
3
yields the matrix product formula (
8
) of the unnormalized stationary probability, where the operators
X_{0},\ldots,X_{n}
are given as
X_{\alpha}=X_{\alpha}(z=1)
for
X_{\alpha}(z)
defined by (
78
) and the trace is taken over
F^{\otimes\frac{n(n-1)}{2}}
.

In the next section, we will establish the same matrix product formula by the ZF algebra without relying on the MLQ construction.

n=2
,
X_{\alpha}(z)

It is a column consisting of
(n-1)
vertices, and the
t
-oscillators attached to the
r
th one from the top is
denoted by
{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!+}_{r},{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!-}_{r},\mathbf{k}_{r}
.
The indices
i
(resp.
j
) specifies the position of the unique 1 on the left (resp. right)
horizontal edges, and
i=0
(resp.
j=0
) means
that all the left (resp. right) horizontal edges assume 0.
The figure corresponds to a case
i,j\geq 1
.
Explicitly one has

(81)

T(z)_{i0}={\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!+}_{i}\;(0\leq i\leq n-1)\quad\text{ and }\quad T(z)_{ij}=\begin{cases}z\mathbf{k}_{j}\cdots\mathbf{k}_{n-1}&(j=i+1)\\
z{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!+}_{i}{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!-}_{j-1}\mathbf{k}_{j}\cdots\mathbf{k}_{n-1}&(j\geq i+2)\\
0&(j\leq i)\end{cases}\quad\text{ for }j\geq 1,

where we regard
\mathbf{a}^{+}_{0}
as 1.
Note that
T(z)_{ij}
depends on
{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!\pm}_{r},\mathbf{k}_{r}
with
r=1,\ldots,n-1
.

Proposition 19

(Recursion relation of
X_{\alpha}(z)
with respect to rank)
.

Let
X_{0}(z),\ldots,X_{n}(z)
be the operators defined in (
78
) for the
n
-ASEP.
Let
\widetilde{X}_{0}(z),\ldots,\widetilde{X}_{n-1}(z)
be those for the
(n\!-\!1)
-ASEP with

\mathbf{a}^{\pm}_{i},\mathbf{k}_{i}
relabelled as
\mathbf{a}^{\pm}_{i+n-1},\mathbf{k}_{i+n-1}
.
Then the following recursion relation is valid:

(82)

\displaystyle X_{\alpha}(z)=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\widetilde{X}_{i}(z)T(z)_{i\alpha}\qquad(0\leq\alpha\leq n).

Proof.

Consider the diagram (
78
), and let
s_{\beta}\,(\beta=1,\ldots,n-1)
be the variable on the left side of the crossing for the
\beta
th horizontal edge from the top in the rightmost column.
As demonstrated in Example
20
, a close inspection of the rightmost two columns in (
78
) shows that the configurations that make non-zero contributions to
X_{\alpha}(z)
are only those satisfying
s_{\beta}=\delta_{\beta,i}
for some
i=0,1,\ldots,\alpha-1
.
The corresponding term is equal to
\widetilde{X}_{i}(z)T(z)_{i\alpha}
, where the factors
T(z)_{i\alpha}
is the weight from the rightmost
(n\!-\!1)
vertices and
\widetilde{X}_{i}(z)
emerges from the remaining part.
∎

In view of (
81
), the sum (
82
) for the case
\alpha\geq 1
is actually restricted as

\sum_{i=0}^{\alpha-1}\widetilde{X}_{i}(z)T(z)_{i\alpha}
.
Clearly,
\widetilde{X}_{i}(z)
and
T(z)_{i\alpha}
in (
82
) are commutative.
