One can also check them directly by using Example
18
.
A matrix form of (
82
) is

(83)

\displaystyle(X_{0}(z),X_{1}(z),X_{2}(z),X_{3}(z))=(\widetilde{X}_{0}(z),\widetilde{X}_{1}(z),\widetilde{X}_{2}(z))\begin{pmatrix}1&z\mathbf{k}_{1}\mathbf{k}_{2}&z{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!-}_{1}\mathbf{k}_{2}&z{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!-}_{2}\\
{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!+}_{1}&0&z\mathbf{k}_{2}&z{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!+}_{1}{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!-}_{2}\\
{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!+}_{2}&0&0&z\end{pmatrix}.

The transposition of the matrix here reproduces the
4\times 3
matrix in
[
CdGW15
, Eq.(73)]

under the conventional change
({\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!+}_{i},{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!-}_{i},\mathbf{k}_{i},z)\rightarrow(a_{i+1},a^{\dagger}_{i+1},k_{i+1},z)
.
The matrix
(T(z)_{ij})_{0\leq i\leq n-1,0\leq j\leq n}
for
n=4
is available in (
101
).
The recursion relation of the matrix product operators of the form (
82
) without a spectral parameter appeared earlier in
[
PEM09
]
.

Remark 22
.

The diagram (
78
) makes sense only for
n\geq 2
.
We extend the definition of
X_{0}(z),\ldots,X_{n}(z)
to
n=0,1
as follows:

(84)

\begin{split}\text{$n=1$:}\;\;&X_{0}(z)=1,\quad X_{1}(z)=z,\\
\text{$n=0$:}\;\;&X_{0}(z)=1.\end{split}

Then the recursion relation (
82
) also holds at
n=1
.
The
n=1
case agrees with
[
CdGW15
, Eq.(42)]
.

Several diagrammatic representations of the matrix product operators
X_{\alpha}

or
X_{\alpha}(z)
were devised in earlier works
[
CdGW15
,
PEM09
]
.
However, we find that the CTM representation in (
78
) is the simplest
and most systematic, offering a clear visualization of their evaluation and
providing clarity to Proposition
19
.
This advancement has been made possible through the introduction of the
strange five vertex model.

5.
Proof of the Zamolodchikov–Faddeev algebra relation

The aim of this section is to show the ZF algebra relation (
15
).

5.1.
RLL=LLR
relation

Recall that the bosonic Fock space
F
is defined in (
50
).
Set
\mathcal{F}=F^{\otimes(n+1)}
.
For a sequence of non-negative integers
\mathbf{m}=(m_{0},\ldots,m_{n})
,
5
5

5

This
{\bf m}
should not be confused with
the multiplicity introduced in (
6
).

we write
\lvert\mathbf{m}\rangle=\lvert m_{0}\rangle\otimes\cdots\otimes\lvert m_{n}\rangle\in\mathcal{F}
.
Set

(85)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{l}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{Q}}(t)\langle\lvert\mathbf{m}\rangle\mid|\mathbf{m}\in D_{l}\rangle,

(86)

\displaystyle D_{l}

\displaystyle=\{\mathbf{m}=(m_{0},\ldots,m_{n})\in({\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0})^{n+1}\mid m_{0}+\cdots+m_{n}=l\}.

Denote by
{\bf e}_{i}\in D_{l=1}
the
i
th standard basis vector.
For
0\leq\alpha,\beta\leq n

and
\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}\in D_{l}
, set

(87)

\displaystyle L(z)^{\beta,\mathbf{b}}_{\alpha,\mathbf{a}}=\delta_{{\bf e}_{\alpha}+\mathbf{a}}^{{\bf e}_{\beta}+\mathbf{b}}\,t^{a_{\beta+1}+\cdots+a_{n}}(1-t^{a_{\beta}}z^{\theta(\alpha=\beta)})z^{\theta(\alpha>\beta)},

and define a linear operator

(88)

\displaystyle L(z)\colon\quad\mathcal{F}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{l}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathcal{F}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{l}

\displaystyle\lvert\rm{\bf e}_{\alpha}\rangle\otimes\lvert\mathbf{a}\rangle

\displaystyle\longmapsto\sum_{\beta\in\{0,\ldots,n\},\mathbf{b}\in D_{l}}L(z)^{\beta,\mathbf{b}}_{\alpha,\mathbf{a}}\lvert\rm{\bf e}_{\beta}\rangle\otimes\lvert\mathbf{b}\rangle.

The dependence on
t
and
l
has been suppressed in the notation.
We also introduce the components

(89)

\displaystyle L(z)_{\alpha}^{\beta}\colon\;\;\mathcal{F}_{l}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathcal{F}_{l}\quad(0\leq\alpha,\beta\leq n)

\displaystyle\lvert\mathbf{a}\rangle

\displaystyle\longmapsto\sum_{\mathbf{b}\in D_{l}}L(z)^{\beta,\mathbf{b}}_{\alpha,\mathbf{a}}\lvert\mathbf{b}\rangle=L(z)^{\beta,\mathbf{a}+\rm{\bf e}_{\alpha}-\rm{\bf e}_{\beta}}_{\alpha,\mathbf{a}}\lvert\mathbf{a}+\rm{\bf e}_{\alpha}-\rm{\bf e}_{\beta}\rangle,

where the RHS is to be understood as 0 unless
\mathbf{a}+{\bf e}_{\alpha}-{\bf e}_{\beta}\in D_{l}
.

Remark 23
.

Let
R_{\rm KMMO}(z)^{e_{k},\delta}_{e_{j},\beta}
denote the elements
of the
R
matrix given in the first equation in
[
KMMO16
, App.A]
with

m
replaced with
l
.
6
6

6

Indices
1,\ldots,n+1
in
[
KMMO16
]
should also be
replaced with
0,\ldots,n
.

According to
[
KMMO16
, Eqs.(15),(16)]
, its stochastic gauge is given by

S_{\rm KMMO}(z)^{e_{k},\delta}_{e_{j},\beta}:=q^{\eta}R_{\rm KMMO}(z)^{e_{k},\delta}_{e_{j},\beta}
with

\eta=\delta_{0}+\cdots+\delta_{k-1}-(\beta_{j+1}+\cdots+\beta_{n})
.
Then one has

(90)

\displaystyle(1-q^{2l}z)S_{\rm KMMO}(q^{1-l}z^{-1})^{\beta,\mathbf{b}}_{\alpha,\mathbf{a}}=\delta_{{\bf e}_{\alpha}+\mathbf{a}}^{{\bf e}_{\beta}+\mathbf{b}}\,q^{2(a_{0}+\cdots+a_{\beta-1})}(1-q^{2a_{\beta}}z^{\theta(\alpha=\beta)})z^{\theta(\alpha<\beta)}.

The element (
87
) is obtained from (
90
) by reversing the indices as

(\alpha,\beta)\rightarrow(n-\alpha,n-\beta)
,

\mathbf{a}\rightarrow(a_{n},\ldots,a_{0})
,

\mathbf{b}\rightarrow(b_{n},\ldots,b_{0})
and setting
q^{2}\rightarrow t
.
When
l=1
, it reduces to the scalar multiple of (
16
)
as
L(z)^{\gamma,\rm{\bf e}_{\delta}}_{\alpha,\rm{\bf e}_{\beta}}=(1-tz)R(z)^{\gamma,\delta}_{\alpha,\beta}
.
The operator
L(z)
is “stochastic” in the sense that

\sum_{\beta,\mathbf{b}}L(z)^{\beta,\mathbf{b}}_{\alpha,\mathbf{a}}=1-zt^{l}
is independent of

\alpha
and
\mathbf{a}
.

Proposition 24

(
RLL=LLR
relation)
.

For any
a,b,i,j\in\{0,\ldots,n\}
, the following equality is valid:

(91)

\sum_{a^{\prime},b^{\prime}=0}^{n}R(x/y)_{i,j}^{a^{\prime},b^{\prime}}L(y)_{b^{\prime}}^{b}L(x)_{a^{\prime}}^{a}=\sum_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}=0}^{n}L(x)_{i}^{i^{\prime}}L(y)_{j}^{j^{\prime}}R(x/y)_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}^{a,b}.

Proof.

From Remark
23
, one has the
RLL=LLR
relation

R_{12}(x/y)L_{13}(x)L_{23}(y)=L_{23}(y)L_{13}(x)R_{12}(x/y)

in
\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{F}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{l})

by setting
(k,l,m)\rightarrow(1,1,l)
and

(x,y)\rightarrow(x/y,y)
in the Yang–Baxter equation for the stochastic
R
’s
in
[
KMMO16
, Prop.4]
.
The relation (
91
) is a component of it corresponding to the transition

\lvert{\bf e}_{i}\rangle\otimes\lvert{\bf e}_{j}\rangle\mapsto\lvert{\bf e}_{a}\rangle\otimes\lvert{\bf e}_{b}\rangle

in the
\mathcal{F}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{1}
part.
∎

Consider the constant part of (
89
):

(92)

L(0)_{\alpha}^{\beta}\lvert{\bf m}\rangle=\begin{cases}t^{m_{\beta+1}+\cdots+m_{n}}\lvert{\bf m}\rangle&(\alpha=\beta),\\
t^{m_{\beta+1}+\cdots+m_{n}}(1-t^{m_{\beta}})\lvert{\bf m}+{\bf e}_{\alpha}-{\bf e}_{\beta}\rangle&(\alpha<\beta),\\
0&(\alpha>\beta).\end{cases}

In Proposition
24
, replace
(x,y)
by
(cx^{-1},cy^{-1})
and take the limit
c\rightarrow 0
.
The result reads

(93)

\sum_{a^{\prime},b^{\prime}=0}^{n}R(y/x)_{i,j}^{a^{\prime},b^{\prime}}L(0)_{b^{\prime}}^{b}L(0)_{a^{\prime}}^{a}=\sum_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}=0}^{n}L(0)_{i}^{i^{\prime}}L(0)_{j}^{j^{\prime}}R(y/x)_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}^{a,b}.

We would like to reformulate (
93
) as a
t
-oscillator valued equation.
On
F
,
t
-oscillators
\mathbf{k},{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!+},{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!-}
act as (
50
).
We let
n+1
copies of the
t
-oscillators act on
\mathcal{F}
.
We distinguish them by putting a subscript
as
{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!+}_{i}
to signify which component it acts on. Note that we label the components as

0,1,\ldots,n
from the left.
Introduce further the space without the
0
th component in
\mathcal{F}
and the projection onto it as

(94)

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{F}}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{(m_{1},\ldots,m_{n})\in({\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0})^{n}}\mathbb{Q}(t)|m_{1}\rangle\otimes\cdots\otimes|m_{n}\rangle,

(95)

\displaystyle\iota\colon

\displaystyle\;\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{F}}\,;\;\;|m_{0}\rangle\otimes|m_{1}\rangle\otimes\cdots\otimes|m_{n}\rangle\mapsto|m_{1}\rangle\otimes\cdots\otimes|m_{n}\rangle.

Then by taking the image of (
93
) by
\iota
, we obtain

(96)

\sum_{a^{\prime},b^{\prime}=0}^{n}R(y/x)_{i,j}^{a^{\prime},b^{\prime}}\mathcal{L}_{b^{\prime}}^{b}\mathcal{L}_{a^{\prime}}^{a}=\sum_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}=0}^{n}\mathcal{L}_{i}^{i^{\prime}}\mathcal{L}_{j}^{j^{\prime}}R(y/x)_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}^{a,b},

where
\mathcal{L}_{\alpha}^{\beta}\in\mathrm{End}(\overline{\mathcal{F}})
is given
in terms of the
t
-oscillators
{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!\pm}_{i},\mathbf{k}_{i}\,(i=1,\ldots,n)
as

(97)

\mathcal{L}_{\alpha}^{\beta}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{k}_{\beta+1}\cdots\mathbf{k}_{n}&(\alpha=\beta),\\
{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!+}_{\alpha}{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!-}_{\beta}\mathbf{k}_{\beta+1}\cdots\mathbf{k}_{n}&(\alpha<\beta),\\
0&(\alpha>\beta),\end{cases}

for
0\leq\alpha,\beta\leq n
with
{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!+}_{0}=1
in the middle case.
As mentioned in Remark
10
,
the formula (
97
) can be interpreted as the “Holstein–Primakov representation” of the
L
-operators.
Analogous results in the “crystal” gauge as opposed to the stochastic one adopted here
have been obtained in
[
IKO04
]
for
A^{(1)}_{n}
and also
D^{(1)}_{n}

in the study of quantized box-ball systems.

5.2.
Rank reducing
RTT=TTR
relation

Recall that
T(z)_{\alpha\beta}
is defined in (
81
)
for
0\leq\alpha\leq n-1
and
0\leq\beta\leq n
.
By direct comparison with (
97
), we obtain the relation

(98)

\mathcal{L}_{\alpha}^{\beta}=T(z)_{\alpha,\beta+1}({\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!-}_{n})^{\delta_{\beta n}}(z^{-1}\mathbf{k}_{n})^{\theta(\beta\neq n)}\quad(0\leq\alpha\leq n-1,\,0\leq\beta\leq n),

where
T(z)_{\alpha,n+1}:=T(z)_{\alpha 0}
.
Operator ordering does not matter in (
98
)
since
T(z)_{ij}
involves only
{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!\pm}_{\alpha}
and
\mathbf{k}_{\alpha}

with
\alpha=1,\ldots,n-1
.
The relation (
98
) serves as the crucial link between the
RLL=LLR
relation (Proposition
24
)
and the forthcoming rank-reducing
RTT=TTR
relation (Proposition
26
),
a connection that has previously gone unnoticed in the literature.

Example 25
.

From (
97
), the LHS of (
98
) for
n=4
in the matrix form is given as

(99)

\bigl(\mathcal{L}_{\alpha}^{\beta}\bigr)_{0\leq\alpha\leq 3,0\leq\beta\leq 4}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{k}_{1}\mathbf{k}_{2}\mathbf{k}_{3}\mathbf{k}_{4}&{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!-}_{1}\mathbf{k}_{2}\mathbf{k}_{3}\mathbf{k}_{4}&{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!-}_{2}\mathbf{k}_{3}\mathbf{k}_{4}&{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!-}_{3}\mathbf{k}_{4}&{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!-}_{4}\\
0&\mathbf{k}_{2}\mathbf{k}_{3}\mathbf{k}_{4}&{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!+}_{1}{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!-}_{2}\mathbf{k}_{3}\mathbf{k}_{4}&{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!+}_{1}{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!-}_{3}\mathbf{k}_{4}&{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!+}_{1}{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!-}_{4}\\
0&0&\mathbf{k}_{3}\mathbf{k}_{4}&{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!+}_{2}{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!-}_{3}\mathbf{k}_{4}&{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!+}_{2}{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!-}_{4}\\
0&0&0&\mathbf{k}_{4}&{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!+}_{3}{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!-}_{4}\end{pmatrix}.

Similarly, the RHS of (
98
) for
n=4
reads

(100)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}T(z)_{01}z^{-1}\mathbf{k}_{4}&T(z)_{02}z^{-1}\mathbf{k}_{4}&T(z)_{03}z^{-1}\mathbf{k}_{4}&T(z)_{04}z^{-1}\mathbf{k}_{4}&T(z)_{00}{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!-}_{4}\\
T(z)_{11}z^{-1}\mathbf{k}_{4}&T(z)_{12}z^{-1}\mathbf{k}_{4}&T(z)_{13}z^{-1}\mathbf{k}_{4}&T(z)_{14}z^{-1}\mathbf{k}_{4}&T(z)_{10}{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!-}_{4}\\
T(z)_{21}z^{-1}\mathbf{k}_{4}&T(z)_{22}z^{-1}\mathbf{k}_{4}&T(z)_{23}z^{-1}\mathbf{k}_{4}&T(z)_{24}z^{-1}\mathbf{k}_{4}&T(z)_{20}{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!-}_{4}\\
T(z)_{31}z^{-1}\mathbf{k}_{4}&T(z)_{32}z^{-1}\mathbf{k}_{4}&T(z)_{33}z^{-1}\mathbf{k}_{4}&T(z)_{34}z^{-1}\mathbf{k}_{4}&T(z)_{30}{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!-}_{4}\end{pmatrix}.

They indeed coincide due to (
81
) with
n=4
, which reads

(101)

\displaystyle(T(z)_{\alpha\beta})_{0\leq\alpha\leq 3,0\leq\beta\leq 4}=\begin{pmatrix}1&z\mathbf{k}_{1}\mathbf{k}_{2}\mathbf{k}_{3}&z{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!-}_{1}\mathbf{k}_{2}\mathbf{k}_{3}&z{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!-}_{2}\mathbf{k}_{3}&z{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!-}_{3}\\
{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!+}_{1}&0&z\mathbf{k}_{2}\mathbf{k}_{3}&z{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!+}_{1}{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!-}_{2}\mathbf{k}_{3}&z{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!+}_{1}{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!-}_{3}\\
{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!+}_{2}&0&0&z\mathbf{k}_{3}&z{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!+}_{2}{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!-}_{3}\\
{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!+}_{3}&0&0&0&z\end{pmatrix}.

The relation (
96
) depends on
x
and
y

only through the ratio
y/x
.
However, switching to the description with
T(z)_{\alpha\beta}

via (
98
) appropriately reinstates a non-trivial dependence
on the spectral parameters, as we will demonstrate below.

Proposition 26

(Rank-reducing
RTT=TTR
relation)
.

For
0\leq a,b\leq n
and
0\leq i,j\leq n-1
, we have

(102)

\sum_{a^{\prime},b^{\prime}=0}^{n-1}R(y/x)_{i,j}^{a^{\prime},b^{\prime}}T(y)_{b^{\prime}b}T(x)_{a^{\prime}a}=\sum_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}=0}^{n}T(x)_{ii^{\prime}}T(y)_{jj^{\prime}}R(y/x)_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}^{a,b}.

Proof.

In (
96
),
substitute (
98
) with
z
taken as
y,x,x,y
for

\mathcal{L}_{b^{\prime}}^{b},\mathcal{L}_{a^{\prime}}^{a},\mathcal{L}_{i}^{i^{\prime}},\mathcal{L}_{j}^{j^{\prime}}
, respectively.
Restrict the range of
i,j
to
0\leq i,j\leq n-1
.
Then from the weight preservation, one can also restrict the summation for
a^{\prime},b^{\prime}
to
0\leq a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\leq n-1
.
Hence we get

\displaystyle\sum_{a^{\prime},b^{\prime}=0}^{n-1}R(y/x)_{i,j}^{a^{\prime},b^{\prime}}y^{\delta_{bn}}T(y)_{b^{\prime},b+1}({\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!-}_{n})^{\delta_{bn}}\mathbf{k}_{n}^{\theta(b\neq n)}\times x^{\delta_{an}}T(x)_{a^{\prime},a+1}({\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!-}_{n})^{\delta_{an}}\mathbf{k}_{n}^{\theta(a\neq n)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}=0}^{n}x^{\delta_{i^{\prime}n}}T(x)_{i,i^{\prime}+1}({\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!-}_{n})^{\delta_{i^{\prime}n}}\mathbf{k}_{n}^{\theta(i^{\prime}\neq n)}\times y^{\delta_{j^{\prime}n}}T(y)_{j,j^{\prime}+1}({\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!-}_{n})^{\delta_{j^{\prime}n}}\mathbf{k}_{n}^{\theta(j^{\prime}\neq n)}R(y/x)_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}^{a,b}.

Sending the
t
-oscillators with subscript
n
to the right using the commutativity
with
T(z)_{rs}
, we have

\sum_{a^{\prime},b^{\prime}=0}^{n-1}x^{\delta_{an}}y^{\delta_{bn}}R(y/x)_{i,j}^{a^{\prime},b^{\prime}}T(y)_{b^{\prime},b+1}T(x)_{a^{\prime},a+1}A=\sum_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}=0}^{n}x^{\delta_{i^{\prime}n}}y^{\delta_{j^{\prime}n}}T(x)_{i,i^{\prime}+1}T(y)_{j,j^{\prime}+1}BR(y/x)_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}^{a,b},

where

A=({\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!-}_{n})^{\delta_{bn}}\mathbf{k}_{n}^{\theta(b\neq n)}({\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!-}_{n})^{\delta_{an}}\mathbf{k}_{n}^{\theta(a\neq n)},\qquad B=({\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!-}_{n})^{\delta_{i^{\prime}n}}\mathbf{k}_{n}^{\theta(i^{\prime}\neq n)}({\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!-}_{n})^{\delta_{j^{\prime}n}}\mathbf{k}_{n}^{\theta(j^{\prime}\neq n)}.

Using the commutation relation
\mathbf{k}_{n}{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!-}_{n}=t^{-1}{\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!-}_{n}\mathbf{k}_{n}
, we obtain

A=t^{-\theta(a=n,b\neq n)}({\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!-}_{n})^{\delta_{an}+\delta_{bn}}\mathbf{k}_{n}^{\theta(a\neq n)+\theta(b\neq n)},\qquad B=t^{-\theta(i^{\prime}\neq n,j^{\prime}=n)}({\rm\mathbf{a}}^{\!-}_{n})^{\delta_{i^{\prime}n}+\delta_{j^{\prime}n}}\mathbf{k}_{n}^{\theta(i^{\prime}\neq n)+\theta(j^{\prime}\neq n)}.

The
t
-oscillator parts of
A
and
B
are equal when
R(y/x)^{a,b}_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}\neq 0
.
Taking the coefficients of
A
and
B
acting on the
n
th component, we arrive at the following:

\displaystyle\sum_{a^{\prime},b^{\prime}=0}^{n-1}R(y/x)_{i,j}^{a^{\prime},b^{\prime}}T(y)_{b^{\prime},b}T(x)_{a^{\prime},a}

\displaystyle\hskip 30.0pt=\sum_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}=0}^{n}T(x)_{i,i^{\prime}}T(y)_{j,j^{\prime}}x^{\delta_{i^{\prime}0}-\delta_{a0}}y^{\delta_{j^{\prime}0}-\delta_{b0}}t^{-\theta(i^{\prime}\neq 0,j^{\prime}=0)+\theta(a=0,b\neq 0)}R(y/x)_{i^{\prime}-1,j^{\prime}-1}^{a-1,b-1}.

Note that we have decreased the indices
a,b,i^{\prime},j^{\prime}
by 1.
Additionally note that when
R(y/x)_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}^{a,b}\neq 0
, one has
\delta_{i^{\prime}0}-\delta_{a0}=-(\delta_{j^{\prime}0}-\delta_{b0})
.
Hence, the coefficients in the RHS can be expressed in terms of
z=y/x
(see (
103
) below).
Thus, the proof is attributed to the next lemma.
∎

Lemma 27

(Quasi-periodicity of the
R
matrix)
.

For
a,b,i^{\prime},j^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{n+1}
, the following relation holds

(103)

R(z)_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}^{a,b}=z^{\delta_{j^{\prime}0}-\delta_{b0}}t^{-\theta(i^{\prime}\neq 0,j^{\prime}=0)+\theta(a=0,b\neq 0)}R(z)_{i^{\prime}-1,j^{\prime}-1}^{a-1,b-1},

where all indices of
R(z)
should be taken to be in
\{0,\ldots,n\}
for the inequalities in (
16
).

Proof.

This is immediately checked from (
16
) as

R(z)^{\alpha+1,0}_{\alpha+1,0}=t^{-1}R(z)^{\alpha,n}_{\alpha,n},\quad R(z)^{0,\alpha+1}_{0,\alpha+1}=tR(z)^{n,\alpha}_{n,\alpha},\quad R(z)^{\alpha+1,0}_{0,\alpha+1}=z^{-1}R(z)^{\alpha,n}_{n,\alpha},\quad R(z)_{\alpha+1,0}^{0,\alpha+1}=zR(z)_{\alpha,n}^{n,\alpha}

for
0\leq\alpha\leq n-1
.
∎

5.3.
Proof of the Zamolodchikov–Faddeev algebra relation

Theorem 28
.

The set of operators
\{X_{0}(z),\ldots,X_{n}(z)\}
defined by the CTM diagram (
78
) for
n\geq 2
and in Remark
22
for
n=0,1
satisfy the ZF algebra relation (
15
) with the structure function given by the
R
matrix in (
16
).

Proof.

We prove (
15
) by induction on
n
.
Substituting the recursion relation (
82
) into it, we get

(104)

\sum_{i,j=0}^{n-1}\widetilde{X}_{i}(y)\widetilde{X}_{j}(x)T(y)_{i\alpha}T(x)_{j\beta}=\sum_{\gamma,\delta=0}^{n}R(y/x)^{\beta,\alpha}_{\gamma,\delta}\sum_{\gamma^{\prime},\delta^{\prime}=0}^{n-1}\widetilde{X}_{\gamma^{\prime}}(x)\widetilde{X}_{\delta^{\prime}}(y)T(x)_{\gamma^{\prime},\gamma}T(y)_{\delta^{\prime},\delta}.

By means of the rank reducing
RTT=TTR
relation (
102
),
the sum
\sum_{\gamma,\delta=0}^{n}
in the RHS can be taken.
The result reads

(105)

\sum_{i,j=0}^{n-1}\widetilde{X}_{i}(y)\widetilde{X}_{j}(x)T(y)_{i\alpha}T(x)_{j\beta}=\sum_{\gamma^{\prime},\delta^{\prime}=0}^{n-1}\widetilde{X}_{\gamma^{\prime}}(x)\widetilde{X}_{\delta^{\prime}}(y)\sum_{i,j=0}^{n-1}R(y/x)^{j,i}_{\gamma^{\prime},\delta^{\prime}}T(y)_{i\alpha}T(x)_{j\beta}.

This follows from the ZF algebra relation with one lower rank:

\widetilde{X}_{i}(y)\widetilde{X}_{j}(x)=\sum_{\gamma^{\prime},\delta^{\prime}=0}^{n-1}\widetilde{X}_{\gamma^{\prime}}(x)\widetilde{X}_{\delta^{\prime}}(y)R(y/x)^{j,i}_{\gamma^{\prime},\delta^{\prime}}.

Therefore the proof reduces to the
n=0
case, which is straightforward from Remark
22
.
∎

Up to convention, the matrix product formula (
8
) was first established
in
[
PEM09
]
by a direct, albeit quite tedious, verification of the hat relation
(
11
). The proof was later simplified in
[
CdGW15
]
by introducing
the Yang–Baxterizations
X_{0}(z),\ldots,X_{n}(z)
and the ZF algebra,
where Proposition
26
was also derived based on a few lemmas;
see
[
CdGW15
, Eq.(36)]
. Our proof, however, is the most intrinsic from the
perspective of quantum integrable systems, as it directly stems from the
Yang–Baxter equation for the stochastic
R
matrices, highlighted
by the key connection (
98
).

6.
Concluding remarks

We have unveiled several new insights into the construction of the
stationary states in the multispecies ASEP by invoking the strange five vertex model.
Let us conclude the paper with two remarks.

(i) The strange five vertex model in this paper differs from
[
KMO15
,
KMO16
]

(see Remark
11
), where
the totally asymmetric simple exclusion process (TASEP) corresponding to
t=0

was treated using CTMs and the tetrahedron equation.
In particular the tetrahedron equation leads to an elegant proof of the ZF algebra relation
without going through the inductive steps on the rank
n

[
KMO16
,
Kun22
]
.
It remains an open question whether such a superior variant of the quantum
oscillator/CTM approach can be formulated that smoothly interpolates
TASEP and ASEP.
Regarding this issue, an alternative queueing construction,
as discussed in
[
Mar20
, Sec.7]
, may offer valuable insights.

(ii) Stationary probabilities are connected to Macdonald polynomials,
particularly when an additional parameter
q

and the weight variables are supplemented
[
CdGW15
,
CMW22
,
dGW16
]
.
We have touched upon the implications of the parameter
q

in relation to MLQs and
t
-oscillators in this paper.
However, a comprehensive treatment of their applications
requires further investigation.
